## Source & Reliability Note

This document combines three source types of very different quality. Trust them in this order:

| Source | Reliability | Used for |
|---|---|---|
| **Official PPT slides** (photographed) | High — printed text | Part 1 slide content, nutrient tables |
| **Written course notes PDF** (photographed) | High — clean written Marathi | Part 2 page-by-page content, all doses and schedules |
| **Auto-transcribed audio (ASR)** | Low — heavily corrupted | Part 1 intro narrative only |

Passages marked `[unclear]` are ASR corruption, not missing translation. Stray words in the audio transcript (e.g. "Kirtan", "Satta Matka", "redmi.com", "Butibori police", "Baramati", "Kedgaon Nagar Manjari", "4006", "366a") are almost certainly mis-transcriptions and carry no meaning.

**Where audio and written notes disagree, the written notes are authoritative.** Example: the ASR rendered the course framework as "twelve things"; the written notes clearly say **21 things**.

### Slide 5 — The Need for Nutrient Management

> **Nutrient management *means*: the supply of whichever elements are necessary for the growth of living things.**

- Plants require various nutrient elements in order to grow and develop.
- These nutrient elements are found in **soil, air and water**.

---

### Slide 6 — The Need for Nutrient Management: Human Nutrient Management Concept

- Plant – Nutrient – Human relationship
- Food adulteration after the Green Revolution
- Fundamental nutrients

---

### Slide 7 — Plant – Nutrient – Human Relationship

> **Both plants and humans require nutrients for their healthy growth and development.**

---

### Slide 8 — Primary Nutrients (मुख्य अन्नद्रव्य)

| No. | Nutrient | In Plants | In Humans |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Nitrogen (नत्र) | Vegetative growth | Protein production |
| 2 | Phosphorus (स्फुरद) | Root growth and flower-bud formation | Energy and heredity |
| 3 | Potash (पालाश) | Ionic balance and maturity | Ionic balance |

---

### Slide 9 — Secondary Nutrients (दुय्यम अन्नद्रव्य)

| No. | Nutrient | In Plants | In Humans |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Calcium | Strengthening of cells | Strengthening of teeth and bones |
| 2 | Magnesium | Formation of chlorophyll | Stomach laxative |
| 3 | Sulphur | Oil production | Reproductive system |

---

### Slide 10 — Micronutrients (सूक्ष्म अन्नद्रव्य)

| No. | Nutrient | In Plants | In Humans |
|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Zinc | Feeder roots and hormones | Memory and skin protection |
| 2 | Copper | Colour and germination capacity | Skin glow |
| 3 | Manganese | Defence system | Antibodies |
| 4 | Iron (Ferrous) | Oxygen carrier and photosynthesis | Haemoglobin |
| 5 | Boron | Flower formation and sugar transport (in various forms) | Bone marrow formation |
| 6 | Molybdenum | Nitrogen absorption in plants | Bone marrow formation |
| 7 | Chlorine | Assists in photosynthesis | Germicidal action |

---

### Critical notes on the slide content

**The plant↔human parallel is a teaching device, not physiology.** "Magnesium = stomach laxative" refers to magnesium salts used medicinally, not to magnesium's metabolic role. "Molybdenum → bone marrow formation" is a stretch. Useful for farmer recall and retention; not suitable for citation.

**Two elements on Slide 4 never appear in the tables:** Nickel and Silicon. Nickel is a genuine essential micronutrient (urease cofactor); silicon is beneficial rather than strictly essential. Worth tracking whether later sessions cover them.

# PART 2 — How to Prepare Your Own Field

> Source: written course notes, translated page by page.

## Page 1 — *How will you produce your own food?*

I expect you are in very good spirits, and to make today — the next two hours — even happier, not just for yourself but for your whole family. And if people copy this, the entire human race will become happy, will become free of disease, and will live up to 100 years. To make this dream real, I am taking today's session.

Today's session: **how to produce your own food** — that is point one. Point two: we have all heard the phrase **"fertile land"** — what does fertile land actually mean? That is the subject we take up today.

Because "fertile land" means: land from which the crop gets its nutrients. A crop that gets its nutrients does not fall sick. And from a crop that does not fall sick, the person who eats that food also does not fall sick.

---

## Page 2

So we will look at what fertile land means. The next most important thing: **even if the land is fertile**, the crop we plant in it must not face a deficiency of any nutrient — that is the subject we have to examine. When we give it all the nutrients through the soil, it receives all of them and the crop stays healthy.

Then, what are the causes that prevent nutrients from becoming available to our crop? We will look at that. And then, in your notebook, with your own pen, you will draw a plant — and how nutrients enter that plant, how they are digested and "cooked" in the leaves, how the plant expands, and how that expansion produces food for you.

As the most important concept, I had sent you an audio clip: **do not stay alone in this.** All members of your family — grandfather —

## Page 11 — Plot sizing

Because when everyone aligns to one thing, everything gets built.

So now — you have land, you have a cow (if not, you have to get one). And **as many people as there are in your family, that many guntha of farming you have to do for yourselves.**

Everyone knows the guntha: **one guntha = 33 ft × 33 ft.**

- Minimum **five guntha** — even if there are only two of you in the house, five guntha minimum.
- After that, if there are six people in the house, then for the small children, **six guntha**. If seven, **seven guntha** — write this down in your notebook.
- And if you do bigger than that, and a little is left over which you sell, people will pay you good money for it — because such powerful vegetables are going to be produced, and their taste will be exceptionally superb.

And for this, **all of us are going to use desi (native) varieties.** Round-round, flat tomatoes like this — we do not want long tomatoes. We want the really big, big ones —

---

## Page 12 — Seed

— **gavran (native) okra; brinjal with enormous thorns and seeds inside;** the kind of brinjal that turns yellow after ripening fully. And **they will give seed to each other.** I have brinjal seed — tell me who wants it, and in this way we will give that seed to one another. **Desi varieties must come into our house.**

Hey — this adulteration that is going on in our food, **that has to be stopped starting from the family.** And then see. **We are going to produce our own food as medicine.**

**Minimum one guntha per person, minimum five guntha.** Whatever your farm is, **you have to put shade net around the sides.** Dogs must not be allowed in; other animals must not be allowed in — because a food chain is going to be built there, and a dog going in there and lifting its leg and urinating will not do. So put up one really good shade net. Spend that much on yourself. The hospital —

— **your hospital expenses are going to stop 100%.** That much shade net you have to fit.

You have prepared that plot. Now look — this is your plot. **On the border of this plot, plant the fruit crops that grow in your region.** Guava, mosambi (sweet lime), orange, coconut, chiku, mango, custard apple, pomegranate — whichever fruits grow around you, **one or two trees of each along the sides.**

Then, inside this, **you have to prepare twelve beds.** Twelve beds like this I have to make. That is how your farm will look.

When this is being done, the land is simply lying empty. So now, what work has to be done? **In summer, whatever farmyard manure (shenkhat) you have** — I am going to tell you the method of making shenkhat; if you get shenkhat made by that method, it is excellent for you.

## Page 14 — Base dose (per 5 guntha)

**For five guntha =**

- **One trailer** (two-wheeled dumping trailer) of **shenkhat (FYM)**
- **+ 50 kg fish meal OR bone meal**
- **+ 50 kg neem cake OR Orgo Strong**
- **+ 50 kg vermicompost**

Suppose I have **lendi khat (goat/sheep manure)** — dump it in, that works. I have **press mud** from a sugar factory — dump it in, that works. In this manner, whatever organic materials you have, put them into these five guntha and **mix it all in thoroughly.**

After mixing, **wet the whole area with flood water — not drip, open water.** What we call *raan olavne* (moistening the field). Before planting any crop you have to wet it completely with open water.

And after wetting, you have to **take a first crop in it: taag (sunn hemp) or dhaincha.** And **when it starts to form pods, mix it fully into the soil with a rotavator.** After mixing, this area has to be deep —

---

## Page 15 — Why sunn hemp / dhaincha

— **ploughed.**

We do taag and dhaincha so that all the toxic substances present in that soil get exhausted — so that tremendous **living microbes** grow in that spot. How will this happen? Because **taag and dhaincha drink the most water.** The water you give there, or the water that comes there in the rainy season — that water is drunk by the taag and dhaincha, and whatever **salts** are in that soil will come into the taag or the dhaincha.

And on this earth, the plant is the one and only powerful machine that **takes inorganic salts and helps you increase organic matter / organic carbon.**

Where taag and dhaincha have been grown, whatever **excess salts, toxic salts** are in that soil will come into the taag and dhaincha, and they will **convert them into organic matter** — and that organic matter does not remain harmful,

---

## Page 16 — Fertility, and bed layout

— because it decomposes. **Inorganic matter does not rot; organic matter rots.** Inorganic means that which has no carbon in it; that which has carbon is called organic, and it gets decomposed through various microbes. **And when living microbes multiply at that spot, your land has become fertile.** Concept clear!

Then, when you turn it over completely, you will mix all the biomass of the taag and dhaincha into that soil. And after mixing, **you have to prepare these twelve beds. One bed per month** — that is the pattern in which these beds will be made.

Then, from one side, **lay a submain (pipe)**, and **run one lateral over each bed.** Take inline lateral — take the Pepsi one, take a good one, take whichever. **With those, the whole bed has to be wetted properly.**

---

## Page 17 — Cropping and the "no water" beds

**In this month, whatever grows in our region** — fruiting vegetables, leafy vegetables, tuber vegetables: **cucumber, tomato, brinjal, okra, and also cabbage, cauliflower, spinach, chandan-batwa (bathua), tandulja (amaranth)** — and the ones we don't get, **pathri** — all these wild greens (**ranmeva**). **These are our medicines.** Look for their seed wherever you can. If you visit relatives somewhere, keep a sharp eye out — "this is that plant, I want its seed" — bring it and plant it in your place. **Mustard** in the winter days.

So on **bed number one**, plant as much vegetable as you can plant today in the month of April.

Now the **two beds right next to it — do not give water to these beds. From the fourth bed onward you give water.** At this place, **plant taag and dhaincha again**, and once the taag and dhaincha have grown well and big, **you must not uproot them.** Now, the word *uptane* (uprooting) —

---

## Page 18 — Cut, don't uproot; never leave soil bare

— means digging out along with the root. **That is not to be done.** Now you only have to **cut** it — cut the taag and dhaincha standing on the bed, and **use it to cover the entire bed.**

**Never leave the soil bare.** Just as a bald head gets scorched in the sun and has to be given a cap, so also bare soil gets heated, water escapes from it, **salts rise to the surface, earthworms go down, and living microbes stop working altogether — and that land becomes hard.**

So of these next three beds: on the first bed you have planted something, and to two beds you have not given water. Beyond that, **all the remaining beds you have to leave free** — grow taag and dhaincha on them and **cover them up.**

There we have to **build an ecosystem — we have to build a forest.** Just as in a forest trees grow and their leaf litter falls right there —

---

## Page 19 — Humus, and why

— and the entire bed stays covered. Look there, under a tree — when rain falls in the monsoon, that black water that comes out of it is called **humus.** That is exactly what we have to create here.

That is what will **keep our family away from cancer**, that is what will **keep our family away from diabetes**, that is what will **keep our family away from sterility** — "sperm count" is a word we will not even have to hear in our house. **That powerful a soil we have to build there.**

I am talking about **five to ten guntha** for ourselves. Don't take too much tension — just do that much. And then **I will give you the calendar** of which fruiting, leafy or tuber vegetables can be taken in which month in Maharashtra.

So the first planting here is done. Right after that, **in the month of May we plant the second bed** — the second, okra —

---

## Page 20 — Succession planting and traps

— **five or six plants of okra, tomato; three or four plants of cucumber; a bitter gourd vine** — in this manner, whatever comes in May we plant there immediately. Then what comes in June. And as the crop comes in from here — suppose you had planted spinach, and it is three or four feet — **you pluck the spinach leaves, bring them and eat them.** Clear to everyone? Pluck the spinach leaves, bring them, **wash them well in salt water, and make the vegetable — with no chemical in it, nothing at all.**

Now after planting, this needs nutrients. And after planting, **pests and diseases must not come to it.** So immediately, along its side, **fit yellow and blue sticky traps** — four, five, six, ten; put as many as you can. Fit them all along the border where the fruit trees have been planted. Alongside them, **a drumstick (shevga) tree has to be planted.**

---

## Page 21 — More trees, and the slurry

**Make a vegetable from drumstick leaves; make a vegetable from drumstick flowers.** Superb. Plant a **hadga (agasti)** tree, plant a **kuyri** tree, plant an **avdi bean** tree. **Leave this cosmetic living — all the old things of ours, we have to bring back.** If you get an old *pavta* (bean), plant it. **Start from one tree at a time.** If you get even a single seed, beg for it, bring it, and plant it. If you don't have a kuyri seed, tell me — I have it.

**Different slurries — that slurry has to be applied here on the bed every fifteen days.** The people in the house will do it; the women's group will also do it; it takes nothing. **Keep a tank; put all the material into it and stir it.** They went and picked cucumbers, kept them aside — then immediately take a bucket and pour in a little slurry. **Its growth will be tremendous.** I will tell you the different slurries for use in the soil further on —

---

## Page 22 — The nutrition argument (key section)

— **those slurries are to be given only to this.**

As nutrients — now, here, **excess and unreasonable use of all nutrients makes land infertile.** If it is given exactly as much as is needed and the crops take exactly that much, **it does not make the land infertile. Do not call chemical fertilizers poison.**

If you took urea and dumped this much, it went into the water, **nitrate increased in your water, you drank that water, you will get cancer** — so **don't call it poison for that reason**. If you did not give that same urea to the crops, then **amino acids are not formed in the crops; if amino acids are not formed, protein is not formed.** Then which protein are you going to get, if there is nitrogen deficiency in your crop?

**So do not malnourish yourself under the name "organic."** To live, you need **nitrogen, phosphorus, potash, calcium, magnesium, sulphur, zinc, iron, copper,**

---

## Page 23 — Where nutrients come from; Avena Nutrimix

— **manganese, boron, molybdenum.** You need all of it. **From where?** By eating urea, by eating single super phosphate, by eating MOP — it will not come to you.

**You have to give it what it can eat — if it is not there.** If it is there, it should not be given at all. **It is never going to run out.**

Some people use a sentence: *"the nutrients in the land are finished."* Our one generation — what of the next two thousand generations — **it is never going to be finished.** The nutrients in it can never be exhausted; it is filled that tremendously. **What we need is the capacity to take it.**

So I say: if it is not there in your soil — particularly regarding **micronutrients** — I will speak about how to give micronutrients when I tell you **what the "Avena Nutrimix" concept is: 100 ml of Avena Nutrimix per week.** Through that, whatever **fulvic acid, seaweed extract** —

---

## Page 24 — His own experience (cattle) and the guarantee

— is in it will come into them, and when we eat that crop, **all of it will come into us.**

I'll tell you my experience. **My Sindhu used to need this much mineral mixture earlier.** But when I started giving **Avena Nutrimix to the grass, to the ginni grass, to the maize**, after that **all of that went away and my cows became absolutely healthy. The clinic shut down** — because all the minerals began reaching them **through the fodder.**

**Our fodder is going to be produced here**, and from it we have to take everything we currently need. And **I take the guarantee that your land will not become infertile** — because we are going to use it more. **The 15-day slurry we are going to apply — through that slurry we give this, it goes into the crops, and our land keeps becoming fertile.**

One more thing I tell you: **the greener your plant's leaf** — listen carefully — the more your plant's —

---

## Page 25 — Leaf → root exudate → microbes

— **leaf is deep green, the more different substances are released into the soil through the roots. That is called carbon material.** And **the more carbon material is released through the roots, the more living microbes multiply in your soil.**

Which means: **if any crop shows deficiency symptoms and that crop has turned yellow, then that crop will release less carbon material through the roots** — and when less is released, less food is produced there for the microbes, so **the number of living microbes falls.**

So the most powerful thing in this creation, if there is anything, **is the leaf.** And the greener that leaf, the more different organic substances will be released through the roots — **carbon material will be released, and that is food. It is excreta — the excreta released by the plants is food.** Living microbes multiply on it. On top of them —

---

— **Azotobacter multiplies. Above them, PSB multiplies. Above them, potash-mobilizing bacteria multiply. Above them your VAM — vesicular arbuscular mycorrhiza, endomycorrhiza, ectomycorrhiza — multiplies.**

**We start from ourselves.** And when you get these results in six months — if you have not then changed your entire farm, don't call me B.T. Gore.

**I am dealing right now with your emotion, with your psychology.** If I told you directly to do it on two acres, you would not do it. But in six months you will understand from the crop — **so far nothing has been given at all.**

**Eat that farmer's chemical cucumber and eat your cucumber — see. Eat that tomato, eat that brinjal, eat that okra — see.**

The health of the soil likewise has to be maintained; for that, whatever preparations I will tell you, I will tell for the whole farm — **and then see how your crops come.**

When our crop looks absolutely tremendous** — the leaf is deep green, the flowers are glossy, the fruit set is tremendous, the fruit has begun to ripen and has taken on a superb red colour, which is the best of all. **It is precisely then that the pest attacks** — because they came before us, they know what to eat and what not to eat.

**If insecticide has been sprayed somewhere, they don't even go there** — their smell tells them chemical has been sprayed here. And this five guntha, ten guntha, 15 guntha area of yours **is 100% organic** and is growing in an absolutely sattvik manner. **These pests —**

After the crop has germinated, or after transplanting — **if your Sunday is fixed, then do your planting on Monday.** That means even if gomutra goes over it the following Sunday, it makes no difference. Because when you transplant — you brought a brinjal seedling — **it will take eight days for it to come alive.** Do not put gomutra on it immediately, do not take a spray on it immediately.

— has been sown by you, **leave that portion out. Do not spray over it. Gomutra burns tender leaves.**

What we call *shivpalak* — in it there is **tremendous antibacterial, antifungal and insecticidal property.** That gomutra is absolutely pure — it is our Sindhu's, and **we have to keep hold of it** and use it only through spraying. And through the slurry we are going to prepare, **we have to give it.**

If **this Sunday** the gomutra spray was done, then **next Sunday** there is a product called **Combo 300** — from **Vijaya Agro** — in which you get **two bottles, neem and karanj**. Take **2.5 ml neem** and 2.5 ml —

— **karanj, per one litre of water.**

# Extracted Operating Specification

Consolidated from Part 2. This is a summary, not translation.

## Plot design

| Item | Specification |
|---|---|
| Sizing rule | 1 guntha (33 ft × 33 ft) per family member |
| Minimum | 5 guntha, even for a 2-person household |
| Enclosure | Shade net all round; exclude dogs and other animals |
| Border | Regional fruit trees, 1–2 each: guava, mosambi, orange, coconut, chiku, mango, custard apple, pomegranate |
| Interior | 12 beds, one planted per month |
| Irrigation | Submain pipe + 1 inline lateral per bed |
| Companion trees | Drumstick (shevga), hadga (agasti), kuyri, avdi bean |

## Land preparation

1. Apply base dose (below) and mix thoroughly.
2. Flood-irrigate the whole area (open water, **not** drip) — *raan olavne*.
3. Sow **taag (sunn hemp) or dhaincha** as the first crop.
4. At **pod initiation**, incorporate fully with a rotavator.
5. Deep plough.
6. Form the 12 beds.

### Base dose per 5 guntha

| Input | Quantity |
|---|---|
| Farmyard manure (shenkhat) | 1 two-wheeled dumping trailer |
| Fish meal **or** bone meal | 50 kg |
| Neem cake **or** Orgo Strong | 50 kg |
| Vermicompost | 50 kg |
| Optional additions | Lendi khat (goat/sheep manure), sugar-factory press mud |

## Bed management

| Bed | Treatment |
|---|---|
| Bed 1 | Plant seasonal vegetables immediately (April start) |
| Beds 2–3 | **No water.** Taag/dhaincha, **cut** (never uproot), lay as mulch |
| Bed 4 onward | Irrigate; plant successively, one new bed per month |
| All idle beds | Taag/dhaincha, cut and cover |
| Universal rule | **Soil is never left bare** |

## Crops

- **Fruiting/leafy/tuber:** cucumber, tomato, brinjal, okra, cabbage, cauliflower, spinach
- **Wild greens (ranmeva, treated as medicine):** chandan-batwa (bathua), tandulja (amaranth), pathri
- **Winter:** mustard
- **Seed policy:** desi/gavran only; explicit rejection of GM; seed exchange within the group

## Nutrition programme

| Input | Rate | Frequency |
|---|---|---|
| Slurry (composition to be given in later session) | — | Every 15 days, on beds |
| Avena Nutrimix (fulvic acid, seaweed extract) | 100 ml | Weekly |

## Spray programme — 52 sprays/year, fixed Sunday

| Week | Spray | Rate |
|---|---|---|
| Week 1 | Desi cow urine (gomutra) | 10 ml/litre — 150 ml per 15 L knapsack |
| Week 2 | Combo 300 (Vijaya Agro) | 2.5 ml neem + 2.5 ml karanj per litre |
| Week 3 | Gomutra | as above |
| Week 4 | Combo 300 | as above |

**Timing constraints**

- If Sunday is the fixed spray day, **transplant on Monday**.
- No spray for **8 days** after transplanting (seedling establishment).
- No spray for **15 days** on directly-sown seed — leave that portion out of the pass.
- Reason: gomutra burns tender foliage.

## Pest and disease management

- Yellow **and** blue sticky traps, 4–10 units, plus a full border run
- **No fungicide**, even when disease appears
- **No insecticide or fungicide into the soil**, ever
- Rationale offered: pests preferentially target healthy, chemical-free crops; the system's answer is plant strength plus trapping, not curative chemistry

---

# Analysis and Cautions

## The central technical claim

The mechanism argued across pages 15–16 and 25–26 is a coherent chain:

> deep-green leaf → higher root exudation of carbon material → more microbial food → larger microbial population → Azotobacter, PSB, potash mobilizers, then VAM/endo-/ecto-mycorrhiza → improved nutrient availability → deeper-green leaf

This is **broadly consistent with soil-science literature** on rhizodeposition and the plant–microbiome feedback loop. It is a positive feedback loop, correctly identified — a chlorotic crop really does exude less carbon.

## Where he differs from typical "natural farming" messaging

**He explicitly refuses the "chemical fertilizer = poison" framing.** His argument (page 22):

- Land is made infertile by **excess and unreasonable use**, not by the input category
- Nitrate leaching into drinking water is the real hazard — that is an *application* problem
- Withholding nitrogen blocks amino-acid synthesis, which blocks protein synthesis
- Therefore: *"do not malnourish yourself under the name organic"*

This is **integrated/balanced nutrition, not zero-input natural farming.** It's the most defensible position in the material and worth holding onto.

## Claims to treat with caution

| Claim | Assessment |
|---|---|
| Soil nutrients "can never be exhausted" for 2,000 generations | Overstated. Total reserves are large; *plant-available* fractions are the binding constraint, and removal in harvested produce is real. His own micronutrient recommendation contradicts the absolute version of this claim. |
| Breast milk delivers "all the vaccines" | Rhetorical. Passive antibody transfer is real; it is not vaccination and does not substitute for immunization. |
| Soil quality prevents cancer, diabetes, sterility | Motivational framing, not epidemiology. |
| "Don't call me B.T. Gore if you don't change in six months" | Rhetorical device — he himself frames this as working on emotion and psychology. |
| No fungicide **ever**, even under active disease | High-risk absolute rule. On a small kitchen plot the cost of a failure is one bed. Do not port this rule to a commercial orchard. |

## Relevance to a commercial block

The design is for a **5–15 guntha household food plot**, not a production unit. Portable elements:

- Green manure (taag/dhaincha) + rotavator incorporation before establishment
- Permanent mulch / never-bare-soil
- Cut-don't-uproot for cover crops
- Sticky traps as monitoring and first-line control
- The leaf-colour → root-exudate → microbial-biomass reasoning as a diagnostic frame

Non-portable elements:

- The absolute no-fungicide rule
- The 52-spray gomutra/neem rotation as the *sole* plant protection programme
- Guntha-per-person sizing

---

# PART 3 — Soil Fertility (जमिनीची सुपीकता)

> Source: audio transcript, good quality. Minor ASR slips noted inline.

## The definition

**The definition of soil fertility is very simple. Very simple. We have to understand it, not memorize it.**

**Expected yield of the crop.** Take any crop in front of you — wheat, bajra, tomato, grapes, pomegranate, sugarcane, any crop, put it before your eyes. On the day you begin preparing for that crop, something comes into your head: *I want to produce this many tonnes; I want this many tonnes of pomegranate; I want pomegranate of this quality; my tomatoes must be big, must be red, must have no blemish.* Something or other comes into our head, doesn't it? **That is called the expected yield.**

Now, a man with *murmad* (gravelly) soil expects one thing, a man with black soil expects another; a summer crop is one thing, a kharif crop another. Kharif onion is different from summer onion. Correct or not? If the kharif onion yields ten tonnes per acre, the fellow's head is in the clouds. But if the summer onion yields ten tonnes, that's very little — because for the summer onion people push it right to the limit. He takes that onion past fifteen tonnes. **That is why I say: expected yield.**

Now this word *yield* — this word covers **both quality and quantity.** Not just quantity. Even if you're making sugar, the sugar recovery in it must be good. If you simply take the tonnage and the recovery is only eight, will the sugar factory give you the rate? It will not. **So yield means its quality and its quantity.** And that yield must come to us in a thoroughly good way.

> **The capacity of the soil to supply nutrient elements — in order to obtain that — is what is called soil fertility.**

To achieve that yield, that quality, that colour, that number of fruits — to achieve all of it, **disease-free, pest-free, with the fewest sprays, at the lowest cost** — that is what we expect, isn't it? So to bring that expectation of ours into existence, **the nourishment of that crop must happen as it should.** It will not happen by merely dumping fertilizer. That fertilizer must be released **through the root** and must be taken up inside that crop. **That is what is called soil fertility.**

## The structure of the year-long training

**And our training is on this single point alone — and it will run for a whole year.**

The technical things, the entire framework we need, the mindset we need for doing good farming — that I will hand over to you one hundred percent.

Because if I had told you to sit continuously for two months, for fifty months, you would have sat and then nobody would have come to the training. **So I am giving you one day in the week.** And when we take up each single point, that one point will carry your land toward fertility, toward fertility — and slowly you will keep getting changes in your farm.

**So — informative, and transformative.** *Information* means facts. There's YouTube, there's Google, there's Facebook, there's WhatsApp — go ahead. You'll get plenty of information. **But no. We have to do it in our own farm.**

Just as — what did we spend the first hour on? On our family, on our own nutrition, on the crops of our own household, on the people of our own household. We spent that one hour. **How productive is that?**

So now, we have to make our land fertile. So that we get as much of the expected yield as we want. Which is called soil fertility.

## The parameters of fertility

There are many parameters in it:

- How much **organic carbon** is there
- What is the **pH**
- What is the **EC**
- Which **nutrients** are present, and in what quantity
- Is there **free lime (chunkhadi)**
- What type of **water** are you putting into it
- What is your soil's **water-holding capacity**
- Are there **air pockets** in it
- Has the soil **compacted (formed a hardpan)** inside
- Is your land **saline**
- Is your land **sodic**

**There are a great many parameters in it. Don't go into the detail online — don't get confused.**

## Sequencing by season

Those single points that I will now show you one by one — **taking one point at a time, we have to work for one week, two weeks, three weeks on that single point.**

**And whichever point is necessary in whichever month, that is exactly the month in which I will bring that point up.**

*Soil drainage must be good; water must not stagnate in the soil.* If I tell you this in the month of May, will you grasp the seriousness of it? **I will take it up when the big rains are falling, when your fields will be completely locked with water, when the roots will be entirely rotting, when your crops will be turning yellow — that is when I will take up how necessary soil drainage is.** Something you will never be able to forget for the rest of your life.

**This is the kind of training this is.** Is it clear to you all?

**The most important point — soil fertility. Soil fertility.**

# PART 4 — How Crops Produce Their Food (Plant Physiology)

> Source: audio transcript, moderately corrupted in places. Recurring ASR slips corrected inline: `टॉब्जी सिटी` / `पॉझिटिव्हिटी` = **toxicity**; `डीपीसीएच` / `डीपीसीएनसी` = **deficiency**; `मृशी` / `कृषी` = **बुरशी** (fungi); `शार` = **क्षार** (salts); `पूरण` = **boron**; `फेलस` = **ferrous**.

## The sketching exercise

**Leaf, plant, nutrient capacity.** The land is now secure, it is a thoroughly fine fertile soil, and now we have to give nutrients to our crop. **This is the most important topic. The most important topic of today's session — an exercise that will bring a revolution in farming is what you are now going to do.**

Open your notebook, take a pen in hand. The other ladies — if you have to go and cook now, then go. **But the children must be there.** Those who go to college, those in fifth to tenth standard — they must be there. Once they have this — what plant physiology is — **they will never fail in the Botany subject. They will never fail in Biology.**

This exercise we are now going to do is for them, and it is necessary for every farmer. And once a farmer has grasped this, he steps out and gets past the mentality of *"either put it on the soil or spray it."* Whatever happens — you either broadcast it or you spray it. We are stuck between those two things. **This will be resolved.** That powerful an exercise I am doing with you now in this training.

**As I write, as I sketch, you have to do the same. You have to do the same.** I say it again — the teacher you had in first standard, the guruji, the master, the sir, the madam — you have not forgotten their name to this day. Because they put creativity into your eyes, into your brain. **That same creativity is now your responsibility.**

## The plant diagram

Now, the plant. This plant is in the soil. Below the soil, in some cases there is a **root**, in some cases there are **fibrous roots**. You have to sketch it as I have shown. **This we call the root.** Clear?

After that these are the **stem**. These are the **branches**. Stem. And on it a **leaf**, then a **flower**, and from that a **fruit**.

Flower, leaf, stem, root. **Do the exercise calmly, exactly like this. Only then will you not forget it.**

Whatever the plant is — leaves will be where they are, they may be like onion blades, they may be round, they may be triangular; whatever they are, they will be like that. Draw what you want to draw. Draw the flower however you can. It will look nice. **It's our farmer's business, that's all we have to do.** Clear? So that's done.

## What the plant needs

Now what are the needs of this plant?

| Requirement | Source |
|---|---|
| Water | Soil (root zone) and air |
| Soil | — |
| Air | — |
| Carbon dioxide | Air |
| Sunlight | Sun |
| Atmosphere | Temperature, humidity, weather, wind |

**Now, water is of two kinds:** in the soil at the root zone, and in the air — that is also water. Clear to everyone?

Below the soil is the root. Above the soil is the stem, leaf, flower. **The part that lives below the soil can never live above; and the part that lives in the air can never be buried in soil.** `[unclear]` For those which, when buried in soil, the root does not sprout `[unclear]`.

## Photosynthesis

Then in this leaf, the most powerful chemical reaction of all takes place, which we call **प्रकाशसंश्लेषण — photosynthesis.**

To carry out photosynthesis:

1. **Number one requirement: sunlight**
2. **Number two: carbon dioxide**
3. **Number three: water that has come from the soil, or water sitting on the leaf from the air**

Sunlight, water, and carbon dioxide. **That carbon dioxide which troubles you, makes you ill, causes oxygen shortage — the tree takes it in.** It takes water from the air or from the soil, and the sun's rays falling from above land on the leaves, and inside those leaves photosynthesis takes place.

**And from photosynthesis, कर्बोदके — carbohydrates — are produced. Carbohydrates are the source of energy.**

## Mineral nutrients

And then the mineral nutrients that came up along with the water from the soil — which we call fertilizer — containing:

**Nitrogen, phosphorus, potash, calcium, magnesium, sulphur, zinc, ferrous, copper, manganese, molybdenum, chlorine, silicon, sodium, aluminium** — all of these are mineral nutrients.

All these mineral nutrients dissolve, and this water carries them from the roots up into the leaves. **And all the mineral nutrients build your leaf. The whole leaf.**

**Whatever nutrient deficiency anyone shows you** — or whichever book you pick up, or whichever WhatsApp, Facebook, YouTube or Instagram you open — *this leaf has this deficiency, the middle leaves have this deficiency, the lower leaves have that deficiency* — **where does all of it come to, in the end? To the leaf.**

Which means: all these mineral nutrients did not come from fertilizer. Set the fertilizer aside — DAP, granules, all of it. **All of these are already there in your soil.** They dissolved, this water went up from your roots, and up there they built the leaf.

## Why leaf size, flatness and colour matter

And the larger the size of that leaf, the deeper the colour of that leaf, the flatter that leaf is — not cupped or crumpled — **the more sunlight will fall on it.**

> **The bigger the leaf, the flatter the leaf, and the greener the leaf → the more photosynthesis takes place → the more energy is generated → the more proteins, lipids, hormones and enzymes are formed.**

And the more of these that are formed — protein, lipids, hormones and enzymes — **the more विष्टा (excreta) is produced in that plant's belly.** That excreta the plant sends down through the leaf, down through the roots, and releases into the soil.

**And this is food for your microbes.** The more energy the plant released, the more microbes grow there. The more microbes grow there, the more different **enzymes** and different **organic acids** those microbes release into the soil.

## The "grinding bajra into flour" analogy

And in those soil particles — whatever minerals are there, you did a soil test, didn't you? — **those microbes make them soluble in water.**

How? **There was bajra; it was ground; it became flour; then you added water.**

There is phosphorus on the soil particle, but it is *bajra grain*. **That bajra has to be made into flour — only then will it dissolve in water.** And it has to be made so thin, so fine, that it can dissolve in that water. And that water must enter through the root pores that are invisible to the eye.

**Look at this!** Has our water become like that? **The drip emitter hole, which is visible to the eye, keeps getting blocked by our water.** And what am I saying — the fine roots have to soak that water in. **Those roots have pores. And those pores are not visible to the eye. You need a microscope.** And through that path the water must push in — the water that has phosphorus in it.

Only then will that phosphorus stay in your water; only then will that phosphorus remain available to you; only then will that phosphorus build your **physiology**; only then will **disease resistance** increase in your plant; only then will fruit set increase; only then will new leaves start growing.

**But for that phosphorus to get inside, the water must become that thin. And if anyone does the work of making that thin water, it is the micro-organisms present in your soil** — bacteria, fungi, protozoa.

## Ionic conversion

These various substances — through their living, they release things. Just as we release waste, so do they. **These are called carbon materials.** And because of that carbon material, the phosphorus keeps getting ground down. Ground down.

> **That is called conversion into ionic form.**

**And whether you give organic or inorganic — whatever you give — your plant takes only the ionic form.**

**Your plant does not eat the farmyard manure you dumped. Your plant does not eat the DAP you dumped.** Correct? Whatever phosphorus has come out of it — the phosphorus pentoxide — the microbes have made it that fine, and then through those roots it has gone inside.

## The soil-test assignment

So the next assignment I am going to give you — **soil testing. Every year, soil testing.**

**You will discover that the fertilizer you applied is still lying in the soil.** Every year the phosphorus will keep rising. Every year the potash will keep rising. **The more fertilizer you apply, the more it will keep accumulating in your soil.** Because you didn't know this — how much the "flour-grinder" needs.

Someone comes along, YouTube says something, this man says something, that man says something. It's a "*sango-vango*" (hearsay) programme going on. **So: the microbes must be alive in your soil.** If they die, your phosphorus will not become flour, your phosphorus will not become thin enough to enter through those invisible pores.

## What kills the soil microbes

So what is harmful to them? **All chemicals.**

| Rank | Harmful input |
|---|---|
| 1 | `[most dangerous — name lost in transcript]` |
| 2 | **Acids** — phosphoric acid, sulphuric acid |
| 3 | All kinds of chemical fungicides |
| 4 | All kinds of chemical insecticides |
| 5 | All kinds of chemical fertilizers |

**So much acid gets poured in to push the pH down.** All kinds of acids. All kinds of chemical medicines.

**The analogy:** if you are going as a guest — you eat chicken, you eat mutton — a superb tender plate, white, black, whatever you want, a full plate is served to you and you eat it. **But would you sit there** if there was a foul stench? You could sit only as long as hunger held you. **Won't you run?** So for them, first there is no place left to live. And second, something is being added that kills them.

**Copper sulphate.** When the copper concentration rises — until now people have simply thrown copper around. Those who apply micronutrients also add copper sulphate on top of it. But when applying it, what do we do — we dump a 25 kg bag. **Then the copper particles stay at that concentration — and what happens to all the microbes?**

Whereas if you do it as an organic contribution, it is 2.5 kg of copper — but you are giving it a little at a time in the wash, as a nutrient. **Not as a chemical.** That's the concept.

## Aerobic vs anaerobic — the "different houses" concept

So now — what do these organisms need in order to live? **Air.**

**Because the bacteria, fungi and protozoa that grow in contact with air do not harm the crop — they are beneficial to the crop.** And those that grow where there is no air — what we call **anaerobic bacteria**, anaerobic fungi or micro-organisms — **those are harmful.**

Which means: **both kinds of living organisms exist.** Beneficial ones and harmful ones. **But what is the difference between them? Their houses are different.**

> **The harmful ones need a house where there is no air. The beneficial ones need a house with all the windows open.**

If there is abundant air going into my soil `[unclear]` then the harmful ones — **Phytophthora, Sclerotium** and the like `[species names partly garbled]` — cannot come.

**These all come when there is no air in my soil.** But if my soil is open, if organic matter has been put there, then the beneficial organisms grow there, and the harmful ones have no place — **because they all need that closed room. Our room is open — all the doors and windows open.**

## The living-soil chain

**Wherever there are beneficial bacteria, only then do beneficial fungi come.** Why? **Vesicular arbuscular mycorrhiza** — root of the root.

- **Organic carbon** present
- → therefore **living bacteria**
- → therefore **living fungi**
- → therefore **living protozoa**
- → therefore **beneficial nematodes**
- → therefore **earthworms**

**And if you cannot see earthworms, your soil is not fertile. Sorry to say. Work on that.**

Set aside organic carbon, pH, EC, nutrients — set them aside. **First, fertile soil.**

> **If there are earthworms in your soil, you do not need a soil test. Your soil is fertile.**

## When biofertilizer actually works

And then the concept: *I want to apply biofertilizer from outside. I need them to make nutrients available.*

**You need them only when the nutrients in your soil are not going upward. And you can apply them only if there is organic matter in your soil.**

**You can use biofertilizer only when you have already put organic matter into your soil.** If there is no organic matter and you are releasing biofertilizer from a packet or bottle — **there is no result, is there?** They have to multiply. Once they multiply, everything follows. The water becomes thin. In that water the ground phosphorus flour dissolves. And the plant drinks that water, and through it the phosphorus goes inside.

**Phosphorus was only an example — it applies to all of them.** And then this whole chain goes inside. **That is when your water becomes powerful water.**

## Where the atmospheric CO₂ came from

Carbon dioxide is taken from the air. **Where did it come into the air from? It is burnt carbon.**

You put manure into the soil, so organic carbon was formed. **You left it exposed during the summer. Sunlight fell on it. After the sunlight fell, it burned, and that solid carbon was converted into gaseous form — into carbon dioxide.** And here the carbon dioxide kept rising.

**We kept cutting trees and clearing land.** And when you cut trees and clear the land, the organic carbon in the soil falls and the carbon dioxide in the air rises.

### The 1975 research figure

> I read a research article and I'll tell you about it. **If you want to restore the organic carbon in your soil and the carbon dioxide in your atmosphere back to their 1975 levels, and stop global warming — then even if from tomorrow morning you shut down 100% of all current carbon dioxide emission, you would achieve the 1975 levels only after two hundred years.**

**And people ask what need a farmer has of nutrition management.** This much damage we have done.

**Global warming, global warming — big words, big conferences. And the man who actually holds the solution to global warming, our farmer — nobody goes looking for him.** Somebody should tell him these things. Instead they go and hold meetings in five-star hotels.

## Transpiration — and the two failure modes

Then with this nutrient-bearing thin water going up: photosynthesis happened, energy was generated, from it protein, lipids, hormones and enzymes were formed, and then further growth followed. **Disease resistance was created. Its leaves became thick, its leaves became thick and deep green** — as the rate of photosynthesis kept increasing.

**But whenever the temperature rises, humidity falls, and wind speed increases, your plant throws out more water through its leaves.** That is called **transpiration — पाण्याचे उत्सर्जन.** Pay close attention.

### Failure mode 1 — Toxicity (summer conditions)

Suppose:

| Condition | Value |
|---|---|
| Temperature | 45 °C |
| Humidity | 10 % |
| Wind | 25 km/h |

**This is a perfectly favourable environment for water to leave the leaf.** As much water as leaves the leaf, that much more water is pulled up from the soil. And that much water goes out.

But if all these organisms are in the soil, and they have fed the water thoroughly with phosphorus, nitrogen, zinc, calcium — it has dissolved into the water, and that water enters through the roots and comes into the leaf.

**Where the rate of water leaving is that high** — the water goes out, **but who stays behind? The salts.**

And the first thing you see is **scorching at the leaf margins** — the leaf looks burnt, or the margins show **chlorosis or necrosis.**

- **Chlorosis** = a different colour appearing
- **Necrosis** = the cells there dying, scorching, burning

**And what does the farmer do?** He plucks that leaf, takes it to someone, and says *this leaf has become like this.* And that person again gives him something to put in the soil — or something to spray on the leaf again.

> **But in the leaf where excess has already accumulated and is causing scorching, you are sending in still more. Which means still more poison. This is called TOXICITY.** Until now perhaps you had not understood this. Now you have understood.

**Yes, we need it. But after it arrives, it must get time to be digested.** When does digestion happen? **When the rate of photosynthesis is good.**

At 45 °C, what photosynthesis is going to happen? At 10% humidity, what photosynthesis is going to happen? When sunlight above a certain intensity falls on those leaves, **what photosynthesis is going to happen? None.**

So there is no reaction there. The mineral nutrients did not get "cooked". And then the earlier water keeps rising from below again and again. **And where does it come and stop? At the leaf margins where water exits.** And the margins turn yellow and start scorching. **Toxicity.**

### Failure mode 2 — Deficiency (monsoon conditions)

Now:

| Condition | Value |
|---|---|
| Temperature | ~20 °C |
| Humidity | Very high |
| Sky | Fully overcast, no sunlight |
| Wind | None |

**This means the water that was to leave the leaf has stopped dead.** Overcast weather, no sunlight, very low temperature, very high humidity — there is water in the air, and the water coming to the plant is coming from the air.

> **Which means the plant does not need the water from below. If water is not leaving the leaf, water does not rise from the soil. And the water in atmospheric humidity contains no mineral nutrients.**

**Mineral nutrients are in the soil water.** There is no nutrient at all in the water present in the air. **Nutrients are in the water in the soil.**

So the leaf water is not leaving. If water does not leave the leaf, water will not rise from below. **Then how will your nutrients arrive?**

The nutrient did not come into the leaf. And magnesium was needed there to keep the leaf green. Iron was needed there to keep the leaf green. Nitrogen was needed there to keep the leaf green. **If they did not arrive, then deficiency symptoms appear there. This is called DEFICIENCY.**

**And that's the game. We know nothing. All we know is: broadcast it, broadcast it.**

**What is the temperature? How much sunlight? How much humidity? How much wind? What about summer, winter, monsoon?**

In our pomegranate — continuous drizzle, rain falling continuously, drip-drip-drip continuously — and still advice is given `[unclear]`. **You are seeing drip emitters with holes this large. Everyone has moved to micro-tubes.** Deficiency and toxicity.

Whereas here what is needed is for these fruits to reach maturity, for these fruits to take colour, for sugar to come into these fruits — **there, more hormones are needed** `[unclear]`.

## Fertilizer is not instant — the wheat-to-chapati analogy

**And we give it. Where? The concept is wrong.** Because — *I gave fertilizer, so the plant immediately took it, immediately digested it, immediately used it for sizing.* **It is not like that. There is a whole process.**

> Wheat was sown, the wheat was grown, it was harvested, the stones were removed, it was dried, filled into sacks, cleaned, threshed, measured, taken to the mill, made into flour, brought home, sieved, kneaded into dough — and then from that you make a puri, make a puran-poli, whatever you want to make.
>
> **Just as their work is to make our food, this too is their work.**

## Calcium and boron

And through the marketing cycle, until now, what has happened `[unclear]`.

**Has anyone's cracking ever been stopped by calcium?** Whose has been stopped so far? Calcium will not `[unclear]`. From tomorrow we will go into this in detail.

- **Calcium is needed for cells to become strong. Calcium is needed for the cell wall to become strong.**
- **Boron is needed for the cell wall to become elastic.**

So I say: until this concept `[unclear]` — **why do the leaf margins start scorching? The reason is toxicity. Why does this turn yellow? The reason is deficiency.**

Then — is it a deficiency of this nutrient, or of that nutrient? **Which means that nutrient is not in my `[soil/plant]`.**

**Did you actually feel that there was a deficiency of this nutrient, and because there was a deficiency I gave this — and my sugarcane which had gone yellow turned deep green again? Is there anyone?**

Ginni grass planted in *chunkhadi* (calcareous) soil — the ginni was cut, it was green, new growth came, it emerged yellow. **Whatever fertilizer you give, try making it green.** Put it on four rows, put it on four rows — **only when the right time comes will it turn green. That time has to arrive.**

**And these nutrients must go through this water.**

## Water quality and TDS

**Water is life, but it is also terrifying.** You tell me. `[unclear]` Do you go around saying which water should not be drunk? Is that ever seen now? Bottles filled in the car, plastic, heated up — cancer will come.

But it used to happen to me too. **When you were small, going here and there on your uncle's bicycle in the summer holidays, wherever there was water you would go and drink it happily.** What bottle, what anything, was there then? We drank the water and nothing happened. **Now if we go to that same place and drink that water, the water has changed** — and you get an upset stomach, something goes wrong.

**The salts in the water have increased. That is called TDS — Total Dissolved Solids.**

**And if you check the TDS of any water now, it is 100% above one thousand.** Above one thousand. If you don't know it, check it — **I am going to give you an assignment: check it.**

> **What does 1,000 TDS mean? One kilogram of salt in one thousand litres of water.**

### The sugarcane irrigation arithmetic

Ask your dealer:

| Quantity | Value |
|---|---|
| Water delivered | 25,000 litres per hour |
| TDS | 1,000 (1 kg salt per 1,000 L) |
| Salt per hour | **25 kg** |
| Hours of power supply | 8 hours |
| **Salt added per irrigation** | **200 kg** |

And that water goes on — set it, it fills, it cuts across, and he throws a stone into the cane and looks to see if the water has arrived. It's arrived, fine, on to the next section. **But the water runs all night, runs all day.**

**What is the state of that soil now?** It isn't yielding twenty tonnes. The cane stays thin and stunted. Watching it, the cane looks completely lustreless. **Where did those deep green leaves go? Where did the plots go? The sugar factories have started closing down in January–February.**

**That much damage the water has done to that soil. Because the salts in the water make land infertile.**

> **Even if we apply no fertilizer at all, our land is going to become infertile anyway. I have not the slightest doubt about that. We do not even have the means — to put it plainly, we cannot afford — to spend that much money on chemical fertilizer. Our water alone is quite enough to make our land infertile.**

**And until we learn this one thing and implement it in our farm — least possible water, thin water — until we stop sending these salts into the water, and until the farmer builds organic matter back into the soil, our nutrient management will never be set right.**

---

# Analysis — Parts 3 and 4

## The strongest material in the course so far

Part 4 is the technical core, and most of it is sound.

**The transpiration → mass-flow → toxicity/deficiency framework is genuinely good agronomy.** He has correctly identified that nutrient *uptake* is driven by transpirational pull, and therefore that identical soil nutrient status produces opposite leaf symptoms depending on VPD:

| Conditions | Transpiration | Result |
|---|---|---|
| High temp, low RH, windy | Very high | Salts accumulate at leaf margins faster than photosynthesis can assimilate them → **marginal scorch / necrosis = toxicity** |
| Cool, overcast, high RH, still | Near zero | No mass flow from soil → nutrients never arrive → **interveinal chlorosis = deficiency** |

**The practical consequence he draws is the valuable part:** the standard farmer response to *any* leaf symptom is to apply more of something. In the toxicity case that makes it actively worse. This is a real and common error, and framing it as "digestion needs time, and digestion is photosynthesis" is an effective way to teach it.

**The living-soil chain** (organic carbon → bacteria → fungi → protozoa → beneficial nematodes → earthworms) is a reasonable simplification of the soil food web. Using **earthworm presence as a field proxy for biological fertility** is a legitimate rapid indicator.

**The ionic-form point is correct and important:** plants take up ions, not fertilizer granules or manure. The bajra-to-flour analogy conveys mineralization well.

**"Biofertilizer only works if you have already added organic matter"** — correct, and it is the most commercially inconvenient thing he could say. Inoculants without a carbon substrate largely fail.

## Where to be careful

| Claim | Assessment |
|---|---|
| "If you have earthworms you don't need a soil test" | Useful as a slogan, wrong as practice. Earthworms indicate biological activity; they say nothing about pH, EC, sodicity, free lime, or micronutrient status. **Do not skip soil testing on this basis** — particularly relevant for the Pulivendula site, where calcareous soil and irrigation water quality are the likely binding constraints. |
| Aerobic = always beneficial, anaerobic = always harmful | A clean teaching binary, but *Phytophthora* zoospores need free water rather than strict anaerobiosis, and several beneficial nitrogen fixers are microaerophilic or anaerobic. Directionally right, mechanistically loose. |
| The 1975 / 200-year figure | Cited from an unnamed article. The general direction — soil organic carbon loss is a major emissions source and recovery is slow — is defensible; the exact numbers are not verifiable from what he gives. |
| Silicon, sodium, aluminium listed as mineral nutrients | Silicon is beneficial; sodium is beneficial for some C4 species; **aluminium is not a nutrient and is a toxicity concern in acid soils.** Slip. |
| Ranking chemical fertilizer as a microbe-killer alongside acids and pesticides | Sits awkwardly against his Part 2 position that fertilizer is not poison and only excess causes harm. The two statements are reconcilable only if "chemical fertilizer" here means concentrated, badly-timed applications. |
| Fungicides/insecticides as blanket soil-biology killers | True at high or repeated rates. Not uniformly true across products and doses. |

## The TDS argument — worth taking seriously

The arithmetic is right and the conclusion is the most actionable item in Part 4:

**25,000 L/h × 1 g/L = 25 kg salt/hour → 200 kg per 8-hour irrigation.**

Over a season with frequent irrigation, this is tonnes of salt per acre entering the root zone. In semi-arid conditions with high evaporation and no leaching fraction, it accumulates. **This is very likely the dominant degradation mechanism in the Pulivendula block** — more so than anything to do with fertilizer choice.

**Practical implication:** get a full irrigation water analysis before the pomegranate planting — EC, TDS, SAR, RSC, chloride, boron. If RSC or SAR is elevated, the plantation design needs a leaching fraction and possibly gypsum, and no amount of nutrient management will substitute for that.

---

# PART 5 — What Is Soil? (माती म्हणजे काय?)

> Source: audio transcript, moderately corrupted. ASR slips corrected inline: `जुन्कडी` = **चुनखडी** (free lime); `डीपी` = **DAP**; `पिटिओल` = **petiole**.

## Session agenda

In this session we are going to `[unclear]`. I know how far this session will go. But as far as we go — **what is soil?** Just as we looked at one definition of soil fertility, so also: what is soil? **Is it alive, or is it dead?**

What are the **physical, biological and chemical properties** of soil, and how do those properties determine whether the nutrients you applied — or those already in the soil, or those arriving in the water — actually become available to your crop?

**We are still talking about nutrients. We are still not talking about fertilizers.** Only once all these terms are completely clear will we lay a hand on fertilizers.

**So now if I say *nitrogen*, do not bring urea to mind. Do not put ammonium sulphate in front of you.** Nitrogen is a nutrient — a nutrient which nature supplies, from the seventy-eight percent gaseous substance present in the atmosphere, in whatever form the crop needs it.

**Which means even the trees at the roadside are getting it.** It is not the case that it is available only if we apply DAP, only if we apply urea, only if we apply ammonium sulphate. But it does have to be applied — **if the plant is not getting it.** And whether it is present in our soil or not — until we check the soil, we do not know.

## A caution about soil testing

And one more thing I will say. **If year after year you are taking excellent crops from that land** — are you following? — **then I have one question for you: is a soil test necessary?**

It is like this: you have no symptoms at all, you are perfectly fit, and you go to the lab. The doctor said go to the lab and get your blood checked. And there you get a sugar reading of two hundred. **Immediately you are a diabetic, immediately you become a patient of diabetes, immediately you start taking tablets, immediately you are frightened, mentally you are stressed.**

**In the same way, a wrong report can come to you from a lab.** In one lab a nutrient may be reported as very high — but alongside it there may be some other nutrient present that balances it. **That is why your crops are coming well.**

**So how to read a soil test report — that too we are going to learn, one month from now.**

## Assignment

**Today I am going to give you an assignment: you have to get a soil test done.**

1. **Soil test** — when your soil test report comes, post that report on our WhatsApp group.
2. **Water test** — whatever well, tubewell or borewell you have, take that water, get it checked, and post that report on the WhatsApp group too.

When everybody's reports have come in — old ones are fine too — **then a full two-hour session on nothing but how to read this soil test report.** Not just "high, low, this, that" — but how to actually read it, and how to change our decisions accordingly.

## Petiole testing

And after that — **particularly in grapes, the best practice followed is petiole testing.** Petioles are taken and `[unclear]`. **In pomegranate too I have now started recommending it.**

So that petiole, that leaf — according to the reports that come, **which nutrient we should give and which nutrient we should not give.** We will look at that when those reports come to us. **I will not do this in general terms.** Right now you don't even have a soil test report, and if I start telling you "nitrogen should be this much" — that becomes information for no reason. **But if we really want to do this big work, you will have to take out soil.**

> **Take the notebook and pen. Sit with the notebook and pen.**

And if you are watching this as a recorded course and the session on how to read a soil report has already gone by, **you must go back and watch that video.** Because right now I am speaking live, and in a few days this same recording will exist.

## What is coming next

Today's session: soil testing. After that we will look at:

- **What is an essential plant nutrient?** A nutrient without which your crop does not grow, and from which the crop does not go on to set seed. **That is what we call an essential plant nutrient.**
- Which are the ones **without which farming cannot happen at all**
- **Primary nutrients**
- **Secondary nutrients**
- **Micronutrients**
- **Supplement plant nutrients** — a new term for you to hear

> **Supplement plant nutrients: if you give them, you will get better results — but if you don't give them, your crop will still be fine.** Are you following? Give them and you get better results; don't give them and your crop is still good.

So we will look at that too, so you understand whether to take these or not — **and then that decision you have to take yourselves.**

And then we will look at each nutrient one by one: **nitrogen, phosphorus, potash, calcium, magnesium, sulphur, zinc, ferrous, copper, boron, manganese, molybdenum, chlorine** — all of them.

Then: **how to use them? At which pH do they enter the crop? Which ones go together, which ones do not? Which fertilizers can you mix together, and which fertilizers can you not mix?**

## How soil and plants came to be

A seed fell into that soil. From that seed a plant was formed. **The sun-god gave it sunlight. The rain-god gave it water. The sky-god, the god of air, gave it `[carbon dioxide/oxygen]`.** So the reaction took place. **Along with the water, the nutrients in that soil came up.** The plant put out shoots. Its leaves fell there `[unclear]`. From that, more plants came. And going on like this, the whole earth, wherever there is land and soil, became covered.

Then in some places there are wholly ice-covered regions — **plants did not come there.** In some places there is complete desert — **it does not even rain there, so plants did not come there.**

So on this earth certain patches were formed, which scientists have put into three names:

| Zone | Meaning |
|---|---|
| **Temperate** | Cold regions |
| **Tropical** | Hot regions |
| **Sub-tropical** | Both cold and hot |

**We fall in the sub-tropical.** In Maharashtra we have cold, and we also have heat — the temperature also goes high.

**Which means the crops that belong to temperate, cold regions — mustard, chickpea (harbara), wheat, winter onion — these crops we can also take.** And in the kharif season, the monsoon or summer crops, the crops of dry conditions — those too we can take. **Both options God has given us.**

But if we go to the European continent — **there, for six months there is no sight of the sun**, there is severe cold, and `[unclear]` how much sunlight they get. There you will find only certain plants.

## Why Ayurveda is Indian

I'll give you a simple example, and it will become clearer.

**Many people say, why is Ayurveda only in India, or only in Asian countries?**

Because in an Asian country — India — **from Kanyakumari to Kashmir**, if you study the whole of India, **every place has different soil, every place has different climatic conditions, every place has different water quality.** When does the sun appear, how many days does the sun stay, what is Dakshinayana, what is Uttarayana — all of this is in our India, and that is why we say India is adorned with diversity.

**And so all kinds of climatic conditions, from Kanyakumari to Kashmir and from Ahmedabad and Kutch to Calcutta, are found in our India. Therefore in India every kind of plant germinates. Every kind of plant grows. And every kind of plant then produces seed from itself.**

**That is why the most important thing, Ayurveda, is in India.** Because the Europeans, the Americans — there it is a continuously snow-covered area; whichever plants with medicinal properties are to come, **they have to remain dependent on us.**

**From us all the herbs can be produced; all kinds of medicinal plants we can produce here.** If the whole of India, instead of selling `[cigarettes and beedis]`, produced this and branded it, and if the whole world saw it as a **"Pharmaceutical India"** — every kind of herb comes only in India.

**In other countries, to get these herbs they have to build tunnels, they have to build polyhouses, they have to give controlled climatic conditions,** and then they grow it there, harvest it from there, and take it away.

**And we are still stuck in onion, wheat, soybean, cotton and sugarcane.** To change that a little, we are all coming together.

## The definition of soil

> **What is soil? That part of land on this earth where snow does not fall, where there is sunlight, and where whatever sunlight, temperature, humidity, wind and rain any plant needs for its growth is available — and where that land exists — there that plant is formed in a thoroughly good way.**

## Ten thousand years of farming

**There is a ten-thousand-year history of agriculture.** If we go right back, take out the ancient texts and begin to study them, farming has been done for roughly ten thousand years.

In the beginning our ancestors lived in scattered settlements — this one's dwelling here, that one's there, that family there. **Later they began to fear other animals, so they came together.**

At first they used to eat **tubers and roots**. Before tubers they used to kill other animals and eat them. **Then fire was discovered.** Then they began the process of roasting, and started eating roasted food.

**Then: they had taken some fruit, and it fell somewhere; it rained there, and a plant came up from it.** So it came to them — take this seed, plant it elsewhere, and if it gets water, a plant is formed from it.

**So wherever there were water springs, there they began to go.** Then they would go into the valley, take the seed from there and plant it there. **And thus irrigated cultivation began.**

> **From the time man began doing agriculture, the destruction of this earth began.**

**Hard to digest, but it can be done** `[unclear]`. Because even today, when I go to Africa — I go to Burundi, I go to Congo, I go to Kenya — **people there are cutting down huge forests and preparing land where the soil has three-and-a-half to four percent organic carbon.** The crops are number one. **The crops are number one. But for how long? As long as the organic carbon there lasts.**

**But fortunately the temperature there does not go above thirty, thirty-two degrees Celsius.** It does not have our kind of heat. **So there that organic carbon can be conserved.** But there is a limit to it. It will have to be replaced sometime. **And if people there make the same mistakes as us — if they unleash a bombardment of chemical fertilizers — then the same problem will be created there.**

## The Three Mothers

**With folded hands, a request. With folded hands, a request.**

**Whoever has these three mothers in their life — their life is complete. Their life is complete.**

1. **Never raise a hand to the Black Mother** (the soil).
2. **If we have the second mother** (the cow) **— bring her into our home as soon as possible.** There will be no sorrow in the home.
3. **And the third mother, the one because of whom we exist — never forget her.**

**Take care that tears never come into the eyes of these three mothers. Then nothing will ever fall short in your life. Nothing will ever fall short in your life.**

The soil is thoroughly fertile, the cow-mother is in the house `[unclear]` — that much is with you. And as long as that is with you `[unclear]` your mother will not meet an early death, because she is with her son. She sees it with her own eyes. **And a mother's greatest happiness is that.**

When I speak like this — I speak from home, and when my mother sees this, and the comments you write, when I tell her about them — she says: *look, you are speaking to people `[unclear]`, keep doing it. Keep doing it for the farmers.* **However much money you give her, however many big things you arrange — it is not that. But because of this she does not fall ill. This is her fuel.**

**So we must behave in such a way that tears never come into this mother's eyes either.** However many days there are, those days are good for us. **Because this must never be forgotten: she existed, and so we were born.**

**Is it clear to everyone? What are the three mothers? Soil, `[cow, and mother]`.**

## Soil is alive, not dead

> **Soil is not dead. It is alive. It is alive, and therefore it gives birth to plants. And plants are born, and therefore we are born. So we must never call this soil dead.**

**So now this must be stopped.** When we are emptying sacks of fertilizer, when we do that copper spraying with the pump — **the hand should be cut off.** Because whatever living microbes are in her, **you are killing them.** When we put a `[chemical]` inside for land preparation —

**So ask her forgiveness. Wait a little.**

> *"For one year I will try it for myself and see how my farming goes. Whether it pays or does not pay. That five, seven, ten guntha of farming I will convert to `[a larger area]`. Just wait a little. Until then just let me `[carry on]`."*

**Ask her forgiveness like that, and then start the work. Talk to the soil. Live with her.**

Many of you, when you watch my videos, will have seen there is no chappal on my feet. **I wear chappals when necessary. When she is not heated, when I am not going out in the sun, then I don't need chappals.** Because she is my mother.

**As long as we exist, we will not stop killing her.** And we have killed her `[unclear]`.

**Because she gave us a hundred, a hundred tonnes of sugarcane — and we put two or three floods of water into her.** We got it free; we got irrigation water. So we poured it in. **And that killed the soil.** Air also needs to go into her, and **we blocked her nose too.**

And she is not ploughed `[properly]` — we go over her with `[high]` HP tractors `[unclear]`. **No large stones must remain.** And earlier we would run a roller over it and it wouldn't break. **So we invented the rotavator. We made a machine to make her fine.**

**But those were her natural processes.** The organic matter in her, the microbes in her, the earthworms in her — **they were the ones making her fine.**

> **The soil that had become hard, with twenty-two percent free lime (chunkhadi) — within two years, when I walk on it barefoot, it now feels to me like a mattress.**

**These are the results I have got. To give you this result, this whole programme is running.**

## She is not infertile — she is ill

**So this soil that is hers — do not consider her infertile. Do not consider her dead. Consider now that she is ill. She is ill.**

> **And she only needs a caring hand. She does not want expense. She does not want expense — she only needs a caring hand.**

Now the hand I am going to give you — you need to have that for her. And then the things that have to be done, I will tell you in one post. **Start doing a few things. Automatically the life within her will grow.**

## We are part of nature

**I say it again: everything we eat — we are the part of this nature. We are a piece of this creation.**

> **Whatever kind of soil, that kind of water. Because the water that goes into your `[well]` passes through her.**
>
> **The more polluted the soil, the more polluted the water. And the more polluted the water and soil, the more polluted your food will be. And the more polluted water and polluted food you put into your body, the sooner you will die.**
>
> **This is the rule. This is the rule.**

**And until we understand this, we will not stop committing this violence against her.**

**So the most important thing is that this soil is not dead — she is alive. We have to give her a rebirth. We have to activate her again. We have to bring those living microbes back into her.**

## The human gut parallel

**In our stomach there are one kilogram two hundred grams to one kilogram four hundred grams of living microbes.** Living microbes in our stomach.

**Everything we eat — who digests all of it? Those living microbes.** They create enzymes there, they create digestive juices, and those digestive juices decompose it — that is, digest it. **And then that energy goes into our blood.** And whatever is bad in it starts being expelled when we go to the bathroom in the morning.

**And the work of `[digesting]` that — the septic tank — that is entirely done by those microbes.**

---

# Analysis — Part 5

## The genuinely useful items

**"Nitrogen is not urea."** Separating the nutrient from the carrier is the correct pedagogical move, and it is what lets him later argue about form and timing rather than brand. The 78% atmospheric N point and roadside trees is a fair illustration of biological fixation and atmospheric deposition.

**The soil-test caution is unusually honest.** Most trainers push testing unconditionally. His point — that a single flagged value can be balanced by another nutrient, and that a scary number triggers unnecessary intervention — is the correct framing. **Read the report as a system, not as a list of red flags.**

**Petiole testing in pomegranate.** Standard practice in grapes, less common in pomegranate in India. Worth noting: if he develops this later it is directly applicable to your Bhagwa block, and tissue testing is generally a better guide to actual uptake than soil testing alone, especially in calcareous soil where soil-extractable values overstate availability.

**The 22% free lime recovery claim** is the single most relevant line in this session for Pulivendula. If he genuinely moved a soil at 22% CaCO₃ from hardset to friable in two years through organic matter alone, that is a strong claim — and calcareous soil is precisely your situation. **Worth asking him for the before/after soil reports.** Treat it as a claim to verify, not a demonstrated result.

## Where to be careful

| Claim | Assessment |
|---|---|
| "Ayurveda is Indian because India has every climate" | Climatic diversity is real; the causal story is a nationalist framing rather than an argument. Europe and the Americas have their own extensive medicinal flora and traditions. |
| "Pharmaceutical India" / herbs only grow in India | Overstated. Many Ayurvedic herbs are cultivated commercially outside India. The underlying point — India under-monetises its biodiversity — is arguable but not established here. |
| Agriculture = destruction of earth from 10,000 years ago | Rhetorically effective, historically crude. Some long-lived agricultural systems (terracing, paddy, agroforestry) have been carbon-positive for centuries. |
| Gut microbiota mass of 1.2–1.4 kg | High end of published estimates; commonly cited figures are lower and vary widely. Fine as an illustration, not as a number. |
| "The hand should be cut off" when spreading fertilizer | Rhetoric. Note the tension with his own Part 2 position that fertilizer is not poison and only misuse harms. |
| Rotavator framed as a substitute for biology | Directionally right — heavy repeated rotavation does destroy structure and burn organic matter. But some primary tillage is legitimate; the argument is against frequency and depth, not the machine as such. |

## Action items from this session

1. **Soil test** — full panel, not just NPK. Insist on organic carbon, pH, EC, free lime (CaCO₃ %), CEC, and the full micronutrient set.
2. **Irrigation water test** — EC, TDS, SAR, RSC, chloride, boron. This connects directly to the Part 4 salt-loading arithmetic and is the higher-priority test of the two for a semi-arid site.
3. **Petiole/leaf sampling protocol** — worth establishing a baseline before the pomegranate planting, so you have a reference series rather than a single reading.
4. **Ask for his 22% free-lime case data** — before/after reports, timeframe, inputs used.

---

# PART 6 — Soil Physical Properties (जमीन: भौतिक गुणधर्म)
