> Sources: PPT slides (high reliability) + audio transcript (moderately corrupted). ASR slips corrected inline: `शाळे` / `शाळ` = **क्षार** (salts); `मोलावा` = **ओलावा** (moisture); `वणा` = **ओढा** (stream/nala); `नामन्या` / `नामजद` = **नांगर** (plough); `बुडक्या` = knee-deep.

## 6.1 PPT Slides

### Slide — The plant's fundamental need: LAND

**जमीन (Land / Soil)** divides into three property sets:

| Property set | Marathi |
|---|---|
| **Physical properties** | भौतिक गुणधर्म |
| **Biological properties** | जैविक गुणधर्म |
| **Chemical properties** | रासायनिक गुणधर्म |

---

### Slide — Physical properties, expanded

| Physical property | Marathi | Governs |
|---|---|---|
| **Soil texture** | मातीचा पोत | Infiltration |
| **Soil structure** | मातीची रचना | Percolation |
| **Soil colour** | मातीचा रंग | Saturation |
| | | Vaapsa (field capacity / air-moisture balance) |
| | | Leaching |

**And overarching all of them: water-holding capacity (पाणी धरून ठेवण्याची क्षमता).**

---

### Slide — Soil colour (मातीचा रंग)

> **Soil colour is indicated by the quantity of minerals and organic matter present in the land.**

**The various shades of soil:**

| Marathi | English |
|---|---|
| काळा | Black |
| तपकिरी | Brown |
| लाल | Red |
| राखाडी | Grey |
| पांढरा | White |

---

### Slide — Water-holding capacity

> **The water that remains collected in the cavities / pores of the soil after water has been applied to the land is called the soil's water-holding capacity.**
>
> **Medium to heavy soils have a greater water-holding capacity than light soils.**
>
> *Primarily depends on Soil texture and Organic Matter.*

*Visual: wetting-pattern diagram comparing Sand (narrow, deep plume), Loam (balanced), Clay (wide, shallow plume).*

---

### Slide — The five physical processes, defined

| Term | Definition (as given on the slide) |
|---|---|
| **Infiltration** (इनफिल्ट्रेशन) | The entry of water into the land through the upper layer of the surrounding soil |
| **Percolation** (परक्युलेशन) | The downward movement of water, due to gravity, through soil that is fully or nearly fully saturated with water |
| **Saturation** (सॅच्युरेशन) | All the pores present in the soil becoming filled with water |
| **Vaapsa** (वापसा) | Air being present in the free pores of the soil, while water/moisture is present in the finer pores |
| **Leaching** (लीचिंग) | The movement of nutrient-bearing and salt-bearing water below the root zone |

> **Note on वापसा:** this is a Marathi/Hindi agronomic term with no clean English equivalent. It denotes the ideal condition in which macropores hold air and micropores hold water simultaneously — roughly *field capacity with adequate aeration*. It is arguably the single most important concept on this slide.

---

## 6.2 Session Transcript

### Framing

The remaining physical, biological and chemical properties — we will look at these one page at a time, in complete shortcut form. **But you must note it down.** And then, as I told you, we will take up one point at a time and speak for two hours on each. **Once you have all this technical knowledge, how to implement it — we will keep speaking about that afterwards.**

### Physical properties you can see

Now the physical properties: **soil texture, soil structure, soil colour.** `[unclear]` — we can see them.

How is the soil, is the soil `[loose]`, are the soil clods `[unclear]`, are there too many stones in the soil, **has the soil become compacted.** All of this we can see.

### Soil colour and parent rock

After that the most important thing: **what is the colour of our soil? What is the colour of our soil?**

> **Your soil has been made from some rock. Whatever nutrients are in that rock, those same nutrients will be in that soil.** Is that clear? The minerals in the rock from which your soil was made — those same minerals, which you call nutrients, are what will be in your soil.

### The 200-year figure

> **But to form one inch of soil layer from rock — what is called the fertile layer — takes two hundred years. Two hundred years. One inch of soil.**

**Now think about it — are we not demons?** As soon as `[we can afford it]` we bring in a poclain: *do our levelling.* Come on — soil from here to there, soil from there to here.

### Rainfall pattern change and topsoil loss

After that — **the rain that used to come would come drip, drip, drip.** Do you know how today's rain comes? **Like water tipped out of a pot. Three inches of rain in two hours.**

**After three inches of rain that heavy falls, will that soil take that much water inside?** The answer does not come. **Then if that much rain falls, where will that water go? It will run off.**

**And while running off, what will it take with it? The very topmost fertile layer** — through silt, it will wash out and go out of your field.

**And you say, "I am doing farming."**

- We have not kept bunds. We have not kept bunds.
- Water should be held in our field.
- The fertilizer of our field, the fertile soil of our field should stay in our field.
- **It should not go to the stream. It should not go to the river.**
- **We have no measure in place at all for this.**

### The destruction of drainage lines

**And on top of that we did even more wrong things.** Wherever your streams (*odhe*) were, water stopped coming to those streams because the rainfall reduced. Where ponds had been built, others acquired them. **The ponds were filled in. Farming started there.** Because of that, water no longer collects in the ponds, and that same water then starts flowing in your stream.

**But how much of the stream did we leave? A stream that was a hundred feet has been brought down to ten feet.**

**And the thing that feels worst:** in that stream, your own government officers are granting permission for metalling, and government money is being used to metal and pave the streambed.

> **There is no sin greater than this — because drainage is the most important property that land has. Water must be able to leave from there.**
>
> **If water does not leave your soil through drainage, then your salts are going to come and be deposited there. And where salts get deposited, that land of yours will no longer be of any use.**

**This is how bad a situation we are creating. And we call this development.**

*"No, no, these people need a road to travel on. We have to arrange for those people to travel."* **There is no other arrangement.**

`[He then argues, with heavy sarcasm and some ASR corruption, that the streambeds should be left to flood, citing Kedarnath and Uttarakhand, and the rains in Rajasthan and Gujarat, where the rivers that had been built over reclaimed their own space.]`

**So we are changing the physical properties of that soil.**

### The five terms

In that soil: **infiltration, percolation, saturation, vaapsa and leaching.** Understood? The words are English. There are Marathi words too. **Take whichever you find comfortable.**

#### Infiltration

**Infiltration means:** if you give water, that water goes straight down. **It does not spread laterally.** Or if you give flood water, that water goes straight down. That water does not move forward quickly. **That is, the water going down forcefully into the soil. The water does not spread.**

**Sandy land, murmad land, light land** — this kind of land has a **high infiltration rate.**

> **And in soil where the infiltration rate is high, fertilizers leach out. Leaching means the fertilizers go down along with that water, and the nutrition of that crop will not be good.**

#### Percolation

**Percolation means:** where your soil has become wet, moisture is sent into the dry soil beside it. **That chain will percolate.**

**Sometimes you will have seen** — you have your orchard with trees, and if you go into the plot early in the morning on a winter day, you will see the soil has become wet `[from lateral movement]`.

#### Saturation and root disease

`[Partly corrupted section on controlled conditions and hydroponics.]`

> **But if there is a continuous saturation condition in the soil — if it is kept completely filled with water — then root-rot diseases come to your crops. Your roots rot. That is what we call damping-off or wilt.**

**And we put that down to fungus** `[and treat it as such]`.

**So what is the first thing to learn? Creating a good condition in our soil so that disease does not come.**

### Matching crop to soil

**And if it is going to be like that, then the crop planted in that soil should be one that gets fewer root and collar diseases.**

`[Corrupted passage; the argument appears to be: he does not dislike black soil, but he selects it deliberately — in drought years the water-holding helps, in flood years it hurts.]`

> **How important this is — the selection of the soil. Which crop do I want to take?**

**The more leaves, the bigger the leaves, the more water goes out.**

> **For any crop: the bigger the leaf, the longer the leaf, the deeper the green colour of the leaf — the more water that crop needs.**

**So that crop should be taken in soil whose capacity to hold water is good.**

**And crops with small leaves, crops with thorns — those crops need less and less water.** Where there is no capacity to hold water, where water goes straight down — you can take that kind of crop there.

### The plant drinks moisture, not fertilizer

Whatever you have applied — **let it be the grain you applied, let it be the farmyard manure you applied** `[unclear]` — give the crops whatever you like.

> **But it dissolves in your water, and it is that water your plant drinks. It is that moisture your plant drinks. Remember this.**
>
> **If that moisture goes outside the root zone, it is wasted. And when the moisture should have been at the root zone, if it is not there, then your crop will fall short of water.**

**So — is your crop a low-water drinker or a high-water drinker? And accordingly, what are the physical properties of your land? How does it hold water?**

### Sodic vs calcareous soils

Some soils are such that **when summer comes, even a plough will not go in.** And if the plough does go in, `[it brings up clods that ride over the surface]`.

**But once the rain comes and that soil opens up, it becomes so loose that even when you walk in it you sink in knee-deep.**

> **Soils in which sodium is dominant behave in this way. Soils in which calcium and magnesium are higher behave differently.**

**So look at the report** — or rather, I will tell you how to read the report. **If the land is like this, then we will `[design accordingly]`.**

*"I have read a horoscope, I have cast the birth chart and I am reading that chart"* — and you feel this man is a tremendous fortune-teller. **But it is all known information: which ground is where, what is where, how** `[unclear]` **— it is all known about the land. It is not difficult.** That is what a DSS does — **that is what our Farm DSS is for.**

### How much can you actually change?

> **So the physical properties of your land are the most important of all. And you can change them to some extent. To some extent you can change them — you cannot change them completely.**

**Suppose calcium and magnesium are the highest in your land.** Or suppose **sodium is the highest in the land.** In such land, in summer the ground is enormously hard, water does not `[enter]`, and once rain falls it becomes loose.

> **In such land, what will you do about all of it? How will you improve that land? You cannot improve it. How much organic material are you going to put in there? Keep that in mind.**
>
> **So to some extent you can improve — in `[degrees]`.**

**Take my own case. My land has twenty-five percent** `[free lime]`. **Now at twenty-five percent** `[unclear]` **do not even farm there. The leaves do not turn yellow — they turn white. White.**

### Know your limitations

**And if some man says to me: no, your sugarcane yield per** `[acre]` **is low, you should be taking a higher sugarcane yield —**

**And if that man is a good man, if he is a knowledgeable man, and he is giving me advice — then I will say: come to twenty-five percent free lime.** *Your temperature is not as high as ours. Your monsoon conditions are excellent for sugarcane. We are people of a dry region. Wherever it rains for us, we come from a drought area.*

> **Take note of this. Whom should you compete with? Healthy competition should be done among our own** `[peers, in the same conditions]`**. We must know our limitations.**

**What I am telling you in fine detail: anyone can come along for no reason and show us something. And on that pretext — medicines, fertilizers. Your farming will never improve. Keep that much in mind. You will spend a great deal of money.**

---

# Analysis — Part 6

## The best-structured session so far

The slides are clean and technically standard. The five-term framework (infiltration → percolation → saturation → vaapsa → leaching) is the correct sequence for teaching soil-water behaviour, and defining **vaapsa** explicitly as *air in macropores, water in micropores* is the sharpest definition in the whole course. That is essentially field capacity plus adequate aeration, and it is the condition every irrigation decision should be aiming at.

## The strongest arguments

**Parent rock determines nutrient endowment.** Correct, and it ties back to his Part 4 claim that Indian soils are inherently nutrient-rich — the black/white/red colour-to-mineral mapping is a simplification but a defensible field heuristic.

**One inch of topsoil = 200 years.** In the accepted range for pedogenesis, and a fair argument against casual poclain levelling. Note this is a *different* 200-year figure from the carbon-recovery one in Part 4; he is not confusing them, but they are easy to conflate.

**Rainfall intensification and topsoil loss.** The observation is real and well-documented: total rainfall may be similar while intensity rises, so infiltration capacity is exceeded and runoff carries the fertile layer. His conclusion — build bunds, retain water in the field — is the right one.

**"Drainage is the most important property."** He is correct, and the causal chain he gives (no drainage → salts deposit → land becomes unusable) is exactly the sodicity mechanism. Combined with the Part 4 TDS arithmetic, this is the coherent core of his argument.

**"Know your limitations."** The most useful non-technical thing he says. Refusing to benchmark a 25% free-lime dry-region block against a high-rainfall block is correct discipline, and he names the commercial motive behind such comparisons.

## Cautions and discrepancies

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **22% vs 25% free lime** | In Part 5 he said 22%; here 25%. Small inconsistency in an anecdote he uses as proof of method. Worth noting when weighing the claim. |
| His percolation definition | The slide definition (gravity-driven downward flow through saturated soil) is standard. His spoken version — moisture moving laterally into adjacent dry soil — is actually **capillary movement**, not percolation. The spoken explanation is wrong even though the slide is right. |
| "Fertilizers leach out in high-infiltration soil" | True for nitrate and sulphate. Much less true for phosphorus and potassium, which are retained even in light soils. Over-generalised. |
| "You cannot improve sodic soil" | Too absolute. Sodic soils are reclaimable with gypsum plus a leaching fraction plus drainage — it is expensive and slow, but it is standard practice. His point is better read as *organic matter alone will not fix it*, which is correct. |
| Streambeds should be left to flood | Emotionally driven. The underlying drainage argument is sound; invoking Kedarnath as vindication is not. |
| Damping-off / wilt attributed to saturation, then dismissing the fungal diagnosis | Saturation is a genuine predisposing factor, but *Phytophthora*, *Pythium* and *Fusarium* are real pathogens requiring real management. Fixing drainage reduces incidence; it does not replace diagnosis. **This matters directly for Fusarium wilt in pomegranate.** |

## Direct application to the Pulivendula block

This is the most transferable session so far. Before the 2027 planting:

1. **Determine free lime (CaCO₃ %) and exchangeable sodium percentage.** These two numbers decide whether the site is calcareous, sodic, or both — and they constrain everything downstream. Pomegranate tolerates calcareous conditions reasonably; it does not tolerate poor drainage.
2. **Measure infiltration rate directly** (double-ring infiltrometer, or a simple single-ring approximation). This determines irrigation set duration and whether nitrate leaching is a live risk.
3. **Design the drainage before the planting layout, not after.** His central claim — no drainage means salt accumulation means land loss — is correct, and in a semi-arid site with a 4-acre block this is the decision that is expensive to reverse.
4. **Bunding and runoff retention** on a site that gets intense short-duration rainfall.
5. **Do not read "match crop to soil" as permission to skip disease management.** Bhagwa on a well-drained block is still exposed to Fusarium wilt and bacterial blight; drainage lowers pressure, it does not remove the need for a management plan.

---

> **Note on the tillage instruction:** he tells farmers to **cut, never uproot** cover crops (Part 2), and here criticises rotavator dependence — but his own field preparation in Part 2 uses a rotavator to incorporate taag/dhaincha. The consistent reading is: rotavation as a one-time establishment operation is acceptable; rotavation as a permanent substitute for biological aggregation is not.

---

# PART 7 — Soil Biological Properties (जमीन: जैविक गुणधर्म)

> Sources: PPT slides (high reliability) + audio transcript (heavily corrupted in places). ASR slips corrected inline: `कृषी` / `मृशी` / `पुरशी` = **बुरशी** (fungi); `पापर` = **copper**; `हुमणी` = **white grub**; `मरूळ` = **मर** (wilt); `नायट्रोफोकस` = **nitrifying bacteria** (*Nitrosomonas* / *Nitrobacter*); `सतीक डान` = **septic tank**; `मिठीर` = **methane**.

## 7.1 PPT Slides

### Slide — Biological properties: the two-by-four grid

| | Fungi (बुरशी) | Nematodes (सूत्रकृमी) | Insects (कीड) | Bacteria (बॅक्टेरिया) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| **Harmful** (हानिकारक) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |
| **Beneficial** (फायदेशीर) | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ | ✓ |

> **The same four organism classes appear on both sides.** This is the structural point of the session: the categories are not good or bad in themselves — only the species within them are.

---

### Slide — Beneficial bacteria (फायदेशीर बॅक्टेरिया — जिवाणू खते / biofertilizers)

| Function | Organisms |
|---|---|
| **Nitrogen-fixing bacteria** | *Rhizobium*, *Azotobacter*, *Azospirillum*, *Acetobacter* |
| **Phosphorus-solubilizing bacteria** | *Bacillus*, *Pseudomonas* |
| **Potash-mobilizing bacteria** | *Frateuria aurantia* |
| **Zinc-solubilizing bacteria** | *Bacillus megaterium* |
| **Calcium-mobilizing bacteria** | *Bacillus* group |

---

## 7.2 Session Transcript

### What biological properties means

The second most important set: **the biological properties of the land.**

**What are biological properties? Is your soil alive?** And if your soil is alive, it is alive in one of two ways: **is it alive with harmful organisms, or is it alive with beneficial ones?**

**Whether harmful fungi have come or beneficial fungi have come — you need to know this.** There are two kinds.

### Disease carry-over between crops

**But first of all — before taking any crop:** if you are going to take crop after crop, then **what diseases did the previous crop have in that soil?** If I take a following crop that carries those same diseases, **then the disease is already in the soil, and the plant that pathogen needs in order to multiply — that is, the next crop — I myself am bringing and planting there.** And then I will have to start pouring medicines.

**Example from his own farm:**

> My orange orchard, my mosambi orchard, my mango orchard `[unclear]`. I took chickpea (harbara) in that same place, and those chickpea plants died — **killed by a fungus called Fusarium.** I have taken pigeon pea (tur) there, and that tur `[was affected]`. **Fusarium made its entry into it.**
>
> **And after harvesting that crop, when the roots and residues are mixed into the soil by rotavation and ploughing — if I then do a pomegranate plantation in that same place, whether that fungus is Phytophthora or Fusarium, or whether that crop had nematodes — that nematode is going to trouble my pomegranate tree. That fungus is going to trouble my pomegranate tree.**

### Why lab testing isn't the answer — and what is

**It is not practically possible** for you to take your soil, take it away, get it checked, culture it for every organism. **That much is not feasible.**

> **So our ancestors gave us a concept, which they called बेवड (bevad) — crop rotation.**
>
> If you have taken this crop, then take that crop in it `[next]`. **The residue left behind there is food for the next crop. And the fungus that came to that crop does not come to the next crop — because that crop is not its host.**

### The onion wilt example

**When wilt (मर) had come in onion**, people were putting the question to me: wilt has come, what should be done?

**I told them directly: do whatever you like — do whatever you like, the wilt will not be controlled.** Not with the help of chemicals; and even if you apply biologicals you will not get control. **Why? Because your disease was caught in unseasonal rain.**

> **Whichever year your summer onion gets caught in unseasonal cloudy rain, that year wilt comes to that onion. This I knew.**

### Un-decomposed manure in the root zone

`[Partly corrupted passage. The argument:]`

**A man with no knowledge at all takes un-decomposed farmyard manure, digs a pit, and dumps it right beside my roots.** It has to decompose there. **Just as it was heating up in the heap, it starts heating up in that pit too.**

> **Just as methane gas is produced `[in a decomposing heap]`, that same methane gas started in the root zone. Oxygen is not going to reach there. Those living microbes will not be able to grow there — and where they cannot grow, root growth will also not be good.**
>
> **That is the kind of adverse condition he creates in the root zone. What did the plant know about it? But the farmer did it.**

### The exudate chain, restated

> **The greener the leaf, the more photosynthesis; the more photosynthesis, the more metabolism (जैवपचन); the more metabolism, the more `[exudate]` production. And where there is more exudate production, there will also be more `[root excreta]`.**

**Then where there is more excreta going into the soil — if the other components that digest it are not present there, then its toxicity increases in the soil.** And the mouth of that crop is exactly there — **the root is its mouth.**

> **Think about it: your own excreta is right in front of you, and you have to eat right beside your toilet.**

**And what were the living microbes doing there? Just as in your septic tank, they were liquefying it.** And above them, others were producing various **organic acids**. Those organic acids were breaking down zinc and phosphorus `[into available forms]` — **grinding them, thinning the water**, so that the water could penetrate inside through the root.

### What we lost, and what we bought to replace it

**The soil food web that was in the soil — we ourselves have killed it.**

- Because of that, we need `[deep tillage]`.
- Because of that, we do not have `[natural aggregation]` in our soil.
- **Because of that, we have to bring in a rotavator — a machine to break the soil fine.**
- **And after that: drip irrigation, plastic mulching** — grind and grind the soil together, form a bed, lay mulching, `[punch holes]` and plant the crop on it.

> **Now the problem is that crops do not come without mulching. But why don't they come? Nobody is talking about that.**

**The rotation that should have been done among crops has not been done.**

> **The poison released by one crop is food for another crop. The poison released by one plant is food for another plant. That is why our ancestors gave us the concept called बेवड. We must not forget it.**

### On the confusion of doctrines

**The government says do `[organic]` farming. In between comes biological farming. And then, there is no alternative without applying chemicals.** So both problems `[remain]`. **Everyone's opinions are different, different, different.**

**We must understand this first.** Hi-tech agriculture, precision agriculture, smart agriculture — **we should be talking on this base.**

> **If the soil has no life at all — if that soil no longer has life to deliver nutrients, to build the root, to grow living bacteria, to grow living fungi, to grow beneficial `[nematodes]`, to build protein — then however much equipment you install on top, however much you do precision agriculture, and however many different pills you apply, it is of no use.**

### Why urea stopped working

**Earlier, after applying one bag of urea, the crop would respond dramatically. Now two bags of urea do nothing.**

**Because nobody told us one thing: it does not work the moment urea is applied.**

> **After urea is applied, it becomes ammoniacal nitrogen → nitrite form → nitrate form — and then it goes into the crop. And for that work, nitrifying bacteria are needed. If the nitrifying bacteria are not there, then the conversion between the different forms of nitrogen simply does not happen.**
>
> **And in our soil, they are not there at all. We ourselves ruin the land, and we ourselves wait for it to recover.**

`[He then commits to teaching a scientific method of making shenkhat, and states:]` **Our own fertilizer factory must be on our own farm** — the same concept as yesterday: produce it yourself.

`[He also commits to posting videos of his own field activity on the group — "then it will be milk as milk and water as water." Not just talk. He says he has done it for two years.]`

---

### The drenching arithmetic — the key practical section

**Suppose the motor ran and filled water for two hours. How many hours? Two hours. And your dripper is 8 litres `[per hour]`.**

| Step | Value |
|---|---|
| Dripper discharge | 8 litres/hour |
| Run time | 2 hours |
| **Water delivered per dripper** | **16 litres** |
| Chemical needed | 2 ml per litre |
| **Chemical needed per dripper** | **32 ml** |

**When that water reached the white grub — do you follow? It reached the white grub.**

**So in 16 litres of water, if I needed that chemical at 2 ml, then 32 ml of that chemical should have fallen through this dripper. How much? Thirty-two millilitres.**

**Now take your area. How many drippers have you laid per acre?**

| Drippers per acre | Chemical required |
|---|---|
| 1,000 | 32,000 ml = **32 litres** |
| 2,000 | 64,000 ml = **64 litres** |
| 3,000 | *do the calculation* |

> **Do you apply that much chemical? The answer is no.**
>
> **So what do you actually do? You drench that chemical here. Straight, fifty `[ml]`. That's it. And after that you start the water. Once the water starts, it goes on diluting and diluting.**

**This does not `[reach the target]`. It builds up the capacity to digest it.**

> **That is what they call resistance.**

**Same condition when there is a fungal disease** — there is a fungal disease across the whole area, and you release some fungicide. **That fungicide does not reach `[the pathogen]` at that concentration.**

**The man who tells you to drench this chemical — Tilt, for example — that fungicide will only kill the fungus as far as that chemical actually travels. Only that far.**

> **But if that same man tells you to start the water afterwards and dilute it into that soil — then it does not remain at that concentration. That is of no help against the fungus.**
>
> **And if that fungus does die even after being diluted that much — then why does he prescribe me such a strong chemical for spraying on the foliage above? How little would be needed there?**

**Is it clear to me? Clear.**

> **So controlling soil pests or soil diseases by putting chemicals into the soil is not achievable. It cannot be done. There is no alternative — you are going to have to make that soil healthy.**

### What to do instead

- Make the soil `[biologically]` nourished
- **Kill the pupae of the pests present in the soil; pick them out by hand**
- **If you can heat that land in summer, heat it** `[soil solarization]`
- **Apply farmyard manure after the monsoon ends — not in the summer**
- Then you can fit natural control into the timing

> **Because no chemical whatsoever is safe for your earthworms. And no fungicide whatsoever is safe for the beneficial fungi and bacteria present in your soil.**

**So if you apply chemical insecticide to your soil, what happens to the white grub?** `[unclear]` **Then where will the earthworms live?**

**Is soil without earthworms fertile? No. Is soil with earthworms fertile? Yes.**

> **And soil in which there are no living bacteria, no living fungi, no beneficial fungi and bacteria and protozoa — that soil too is not fertile. That soil is dead.**

`[He then refers bitterly to soil being sold off under contract — the topsoil going away along with whatever else. "Now, to bring that soil back to life, how great are the difficulties. How much trouble."]`

### जीव जीवस्य जीवनम्

> **So the soil is alive. जीव जीवस्य जीवनम् — one life lives upon another life.**
>
> **If beneficial fungi, beneficial nematodes and beneficial bacteria are present in your soil, then the harmful ones do not come. The two have different houses. The beneficial ones need air to grow, and the harmful ones need water `[and the absence of air]`.**

### EM — Effective Microorganisms

**Going forward I will tell you: wherever your soil is very compact, wherever air does not go inside the soil —**

> **In such soil there are also some *beneficial* bacteria that grow where there is no air. There is a product called EM — Effective Microorganisms. EM1 is available in the market from various companies. Multiply that EM1 and apply it to the soil.**
>
> **Where your soil is already completely loose, you do not need EM. EM works in compact soil.** It goes down and starts eating the soil. **And once it starts eating the soil, air begins to enter there. And where air begins to enter, the beneficial organisms grow. And where there is no air, the harmful ones grow.**
>
> **So to grow more beneficial organisms, to get more air into the soil, use EM.**

**I will give you the detail when we come to application. For now we just need to make this one concept clear.**

### Why copper is the worst offender

**Whenever you pick up that tin, take the powder, put it in the pump and drench** — `[stop and think]`.

**Because whichever `[part of the soil]` that water reaches, it will kill there. It will kill the fungi there. It will kill the bacteria there.**

> **Because copper works like this: whenever that copper particle goes into your soil, and whenever that copper particle gets moisture, it releases cuprous ions. And that cuprous ion kills the fungi around it, kills the bacteria around it.**
>
> **It does not know which is good and which is bad. Just as `[a broad-spectrum agent]` kills everything it falls on — that is what copper is. It is broad spectrum. It is contact. It releases cuprous ions. It kills bacteria, it kills fungi, it kills the good, and it kills the bad.**

---

# Analysis — Part 7

## The best single argument in the course

**The drenching arithmetic is the most valuable thing B. T. Gore has said so far**, and it is verifiable:

- 8 L/h dripper × 2 h = 16 L per emitter
- At a 2 ml/L label rate, that requires **32 ml of product per emitter**
- At 1,000 emitters/acre, that is **32 litres of product per acre**

Nobody applies 32 litres. What actually happens is that a fixed dose is drenched at the head and then diluted by the irrigation stream, so the concentration reaching the target is a small fraction of the label rate. **His two conclusions both follow:**

1. **Sub-lethal exposure selects for resistance.** This is standard resistance-management doctrine and he has arrived at it from first principles.
2. **The internal-consistency test.** If a pathogen dies at the concentration that survives dilution, the foliar rate being sold is far higher than necessary. That is a genuinely sharp piece of reasoning and a good test to apply to any advisor's recommendation.

**Apply this directly to your own drip drenching.** Calculate delivered volume per emitter × emitters per acre before accepting any soil-applied product recommendation. The arithmetic will usually show the recommendation cannot work as described.

## Also strong

**The pathogen carry-over argument and बेवड (rotation).** Correct and directly relevant: **Fusarium in a preceding legume, then pomegranate on the same ground, is a real risk pathway.** *Fusarium oxysporum* is host-specific at the *forma specialis* level, so chickpea Fusarium will not itself attack pomegranate — but his broader point stands, because *Fusarium* species persist as chlamydospores for years, the genus is well represented in pomegranate wilt complexes, and Phytophthora and root-knot nematodes are genuinely cross-pathogenic. **The practical instruction — know the disease history of the ground before planting a perennial — is correct.**

**Un-decomposed manure in the planting pit.** Real and common error. Active decomposition beside young roots depletes oxygen, generates heat and phytotoxic intermediates, and can immobilise nitrogen. His timing rule (apply FYM after the monsoon, not in summer) is sensible.

**Urea and nitrification.** Correct mechanism: urea → ammoniacal N → nitrite (*Nitrosomonas*) → nitrate (*Nitrobacter*). Diminishing response to urea is real, though the causes are multiple — his single-cause explanation is incomplete but not wrong.

**Copper as broad-spectrum and persistent.** Accurate. Copper accumulates, does not degrade, and is non-selective. It is one of the strongest cases against habitual prophylactic use, and it applies to organic systems too — a point he does not make but which follows from his own logic.

## Contradictions and cautions

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **Aerobic/anaerobic reversal** | In Part 4 he stated flatly that anaerobic organisms are harmful. Here he says there are *beneficial* bacteria that grow where there is no air, and sells EM on that basis. **This is a direct contradiction of his earlier teaching.** The Part 7 version is the more accurate one; the Part 4 version was the oversimplification. |
| EM "eats the soil" and lets air in | Mechanistically loose. EM-type consortia may improve aggregation over time via organic matter processing, but they do not create macroporosity by consuming soil. **Evidence for EM efficacy is mixed and much of the supporting literature is weak.** Treat as a product recommendation, not established science. |
| "Nitrifying bacteria are not there at all in our soil" | Overstated. Nitrifiers are near-ubiquitous; their activity is suppressed by low pH, low organic matter, and waterlogging rather than absent. The direction is right, the absolutism is not. |
| "Do whatever you like, onion wilt will not be controlled" | Fatalistic. Weather is a major driver, but variety selection, drainage, seed treatment and rotation all measurably shift incidence. |
| "Chemicals in soil cannot control soil pests/disease" | His arithmetic proves that *drench-then-dilute* fails. It does not prove that correctly-dosed soil application fails. The honest conclusion is that the common practice is wrong, not that the category is impossible. |
| Soil solarization recommended | Legitimate and effective — but note it kills beneficial organisms too, which sits awkwardly beside his objection to copper on exactly those grounds. |

## For the Pulivendula block

1. **Get the cropping history of the ground before planting.** If groundnut, chickpea, tur, cotton or solanaceous crops were grown there, assume a *Fusarium* and nematode background and plan accordingly. This is the single highest-value action item from this session, and it is free.
2. **Run the drencher calculation** on any soil-applied product before purchase — emitter discharge × set duration × emitter count.
3. **Do not put un-decomposed FYM in the planting pits.** Compost it fully first, or apply and incorporate well ahead of planting.
4. **Consider pre-plant soil solarization** over the summer before the November/December 2027 planting — it is one of the few genuinely effective pre-plant options against soil-borne inoculum, and you have the lead time.
5. **Treat EM as unproven.** If the soil is compacted, address it with organic matter, deep-rooted cover crops and controlled traffic first; those have better evidence behind them.

---

# PART 8 — Soil Chemical Properties (जमीन: रासायनिक गुणधर्म)

> Sources: PPT slide + audio transcript (heavily corrupted in the CEC section). ASR slips corrected inline: `शाक` / `शार` = **क्षार** (salts); `PDS` = **TDS**; `चुंबन` = **चुंबक** (magnet); `शेंद्रीय कार्ब` = **सेंद्रिय कर्ब** (organic carbon); `पेरल्स` = **ferrous**.

## 8.1 PPT Slide

### Slide — The plant's fundamental need: LAND → Chemical properties

| Marathi | English |
|---|---|
| पीएच | **pH** |
| ईसी | **EC** (electrical conductivity) |
| सेंद्रिय कर्ब | **Organic carbon** |
| सीईसी | **CEC** (cation exchange capacity) |
| क्षार | **Salts** |
| उपलब्ध अन्नद्रव्य | **Available nutrients** |
| बफरिंग क्षमता | **Buffering capacity** |

---

## 8.2 Session Transcript

### The hierarchy claim

**Now the third one: chemical properties.**

> **Think about it. If the biological properties of the land are good, then the physical properties improve automatically. And if the physical and biological properties of the land are good, then there is no need at all to worry about the chemical properties.**

**I say it again:** if the biological properties are good — organic carbon is good, beneficial fungi and beneficial life are all good, earthworms are there — then your biological property is good. **Where there are earthworms, where there are living microbes: will air go in there or not? Will microbes multiply there or not? Will beneficial fungi and bacteria grow there or not?**

**Because of that, the physical properties of the land will stay good. And when the biological and physical properties are good, the chemical properties are automatically good.**

> **Because nature itself is managing nature. Nature itself is managing nature.**

**Everything has gone wrong, and because of that the chemical properties have been spoiled.**

---

### pH — the availability cascade

**First of all, pH.** For every nutrient we will look in detail: **what is the minimum pH nitrogen needs, what is the maximum pH it needs** — only then is nitrogen soluble in water. **For phosphorus, what is the minimum and what is the maximum** — only then is phosphorus soluble.

**Let me give you an example.**

> **If the pH of your soil, or of the water you apply, is above 8, then only three nutrients become available: calcium, magnesium and sulphur.**

| pH | Nutrients becoming available | Running total |
|---|---|---|
| **Above 8.0** | Calcium, magnesium, sulphur | **3** |
| **7.5** | + Phosphorus, potash, copper, nitrogen | **7** |
| **7.2** | + Boron | **8** |
| **6.5** | + Zinc, manganese, `[iron]` | **11** |

**Molybdenum is needed in extremely small quantity — pH does not affect it much. One hundred grams per acre.** Because of that it is not so important `[in this framework]`.

> **For the remaining eleven, if they are to be delivered through water, then the pH must be `[in range]`. And doing our farming in Maharashtra, low pH is not our problem — our problem is high pH. So how to manage that, we will learn.**

**But first you must know: what is the pH of my land? What is the pH of my land? Only if you know the pH of your land can you take corrective measures.**

### The blood pH analogy

**Every liquid that comes out of our body has a different pH.** The pH of our blood is one thing. **If the pH of our blood is 7.4 — more precisely, 7.38 — we do not fall ill.**

And when the pH of your blood `[shifts]` from eating non-veg and drinking alcohol, **the sooner you fall ill.** And those whose blood pH `[deviates]` more, the sooner they succumb to cancer.

> **So if the pH is around 7.4, your body stays entirely `[well]` and does not fall ill. It is exactly the same way `[with soil]`.**

### Low pH is also a problem

**If the soil pH is very low, or if you applied some acid and your pH came down — even then the nutrients do not become available. Why?**

> **Because some nutrients then reach toxicity — they become poison. Like aluminium: if the pH becomes very `[low]`, aluminium becomes available. And it is needed in extremely low concentration. But then it becomes poison** `[and enters the produce]`.

**pH is the whole problem. We will look at this in great detail, for every nutrient. And how to manage pH — underneath all of it is organic matter.**

> **The more organic matter you apply, the more you will manage pH there.**

---

### Buffering capacity

**The second most important thing.** Suppose water is falling from the drip. **The water falling from there is at pH 6, and it falls on that soil.**

**From the moment it lands on the soil until it reaches the roots, it will take two, three, four, five, ten, fifteen, twenty, thirty-five, forty, fifty minutes.**

> **The longer that period from soil contact to reaching the root, the more the various salts present in that soil react with that water. And that pH-6 water goes and goes and becomes pH 8 by the time it arrives.**
>
> **That is called the buffering capacity of that soil and that water.**

**Many people ask: if we continuously use sulphuric acid or phosphoric acid, will our land become acidic?** Many people say it must — if you are applying acid, then surely.

> **No. Because the salts present in your land bring that acid back to its original state. That is called a chemical reaction — and out of that chemical reaction, some third thing is produced. And that third thing that is produced is harmful to your roots. It is harmful to the beneficial bacteria and fungi in your soil.**
>
> **It makes the land hard. The moment acid is given, the land seems to swell up. And once that acid `[reacts]`, the land becomes hard and sticky.**

---

### EC — the bulb experiment

`[He describes a demonstration:]` Take water in a vessel. **Put one rod in on this side and one rod in on that side, connect a battery to those two rods and put a small `[bulb]` in the middle.**

> **If the water is zero EC, the bulb will not light at all. But as you keep increasing `[the salt]`, that bulb will keep getting brighter. The more salts, the brighter that bulb.**
>
> **Which means: the salts put into that water carried the current from one rod to the other rod. They completed the circuit, and so that bulb lit. So electricity flows through it.**

**What is the use of this bulb, this glass, in the field?**

**All the nutrients going from your soil into your crop** — `[when they are dissolved]` — **means what? They went into ionic form. Ionic form.**

**And what does going into ionic form mean? That the water starting to come to your root became that thin. Which means it acquired some electrical charge. Positive or negative.**

> **So the more nitrate and chloride there is in your water and in your soil — we have to do soil testing and read those reports — the higher your EC.**
>
> **And the higher the EC, the more the water will stay outside the roots. The lower the EC, the more the water will enter.**

### The counter-intuitive part

> **The lower the EC of the irrigation water you apply, the more the nutrients you applied — or those already in the soil — will go into the crop.**
>
> **But if you apply high-EC water through the bed or on open ground, then the nutrients you applied will stay in the soil itself.**

**Wow — what information that is.**

> **The higher the EC of your water, the less fertilizer you should apply.**

### The straw analogy

**Water, buttermilk and lassi.** You have to drink water, drink buttermilk, drink lassi. **And I gave you a straw — a small one.**

- **Water:** you started drinking, and quickly, quickly you drank it all.
- **Buttermilk:** you put that straw in and started drinking too. **But a piece of ginger, a piece of coriander leaf comes and blocks it. So what do you do? Blow! You clear it out. And drink again.** If too much comes into it `[it becomes difficult]`.
- **Lassi:** if I gave you that same straw for lassi and said drink the lassi — **you would be exhausted and frustrated.**

> **That is the state the crop is in. The higher the EC, the less water will enter the roots.**

**And when does EC rise? In summer. And when does the crop need water most? In summer.**

`[So the plant is starved of water precisely when it needs it most.]` **And these salts — high EC means high salts — if these salts** `[accumulate]` **in the leaf...**

**And what did we see earlier? The more water leaves through the leaf, the more these salts go and collect at the leaf tips and leaf margins. And that we call toxicity.**

**When the salts become excessive — the leaves suffer. That we call necrosis or chlorosis.** Colour change, or `[tissue death]`.

> **And once that has happened, we ourselves have handed over a doorway for fungi or bacteria to get inside. And the more fungi and bacteria get inside, the more your disease `[pressure builds]`. Note where the relationship runs from and to.**

### The EC recommendation

> **So think: my water has high chloride, my water has high nitrate, and because of that my EC is going above three. EC going above three means I have served lassi — my plant will not drink it.**
>
> **Which means if you are applying fertilizer, you should stop the fertilizer too. Why fill other people's houses for no reason? If the plant is not going to take it at all, and if taking it will give side effects — you should not apply it at all.**

**Whatever is in my water, whatever is in my soil — low-EC water will be produced there and the plant will grow in that itself. So I will not apply more.**

| Parameter | Recommendation |
|---|---|
| **Irrigation water EC** | **0.2 to 1.2** |
| Unit | **milliSiemens per centimetre (mS/cm)** |

**And whatever fertilizer you apply into it, the EC will keep rising.**

| Fertilizer type | Effect on EC |
|---|---|
| **Chloride fertilizers** — potassium chloride, MOP | EC rises |
| **Nitrate fertilizers** — calcium nitrate, potassium nitrate, magnesium nitrate, ammonium nitrate | **EC rises more** |

> **For crops that need less water, a slightly higher EC is tolerable there. But for crops that need more water, the EC should be lower** — because more water and fertilizer pass through. **So there the water must be of lower EC.**

### The TDS ↔ EC conversion

> **If there is no `[excess]` nitrate and chloride in the water, then it is simple seventh-standard arithmetic: 640 TDS equals 1 EC.**
>
> **But sometimes the nitrate or chloride in the water is higher — through fertilizer or naturally. Then it is `[approximately 350]`** `[figure uncertain in transcript]`**.**

**That is chemistry. But those were things in school science. Until now we did not know.**

---

### Organic carbon — "the soul of your soil"

`[After a passage on how residual chemical fertilizer degrades both soil and groundwater — rain or irrigation carries it to the well, and the well water then feeds the drip — he turns to organic carbon:]`

> **To speak on organic carbon alone I will need five to six hours. This is the soul of your land.**
>
> **And if from this entire training you took only organic carbon, I say you have won. You have won.**

**How much organic matter stays in the soil, how much of it decomposes — that much organic carbon will increase.**

> **The more organic carbon increases → the more living microbes grow in your soil → the more the biological and physical properties of your soil improve → and the chemical properties of your soil improve automatically.**

**I have a lot to say on organic carbon.** I will give you many examples, and I will give you various **preparations** — after applying those preparations, the organic carbon will increase.

> **And we do not only have to increase it — we have to conserve it. It must not burn away. We have to work on that too.**

---

### CEC — cation exchange capacity

**After that comes cation exchange capacity.**

**There are two kinds of ions: one positive and one negative.**

| Charge | Name | Examples |
|---|---|---|
| **Positive** | **Cations** | Potassium, ammonium, calcium, magnesium, zinc, ferrous, copper, manganese |
| **Negative** | **Anions** | The rest |

**Your soil's cation exchange capacity must be good. Why?**

> **Because these cations go and sit on the negatively-charged particles in the soil. Soil particles are negatively charged. The finer the particle, the more negatively charged. The farmyard manure you applied, or whatever organic matter is in your soil — that is negatively charged.**
>
> **And on this negatively-charged material, these positively-charged nutrients — the ones you apply through fertilizer — go and sit.**

### The surface-area argument

**Take a stone. Now grind that same stone into fine soil, and fill that soil into this vessel.**

> **On that stone, how much potash can sit? And on this fine soil, how much potash can sit? Certainly the fine soil will give it far more surface area. It will get far more places to sit.**

**Which means:**

| Condition | CEC |
|---|---|
| Coarse soil particles | **Low** |
| Clay soil | **High** |
| Less organic matter in soil | **Low** |
| More organic matter in soil | **High** |
| Less root development | **Low** |
| More root development | **High** |
| Stony / gravelly soil | **Low** |
| Black soil | **High** |

> **So once the nutrients you gave have sat there, a level is reached. After that, they can go. They will leach.**

### The magnet analogy

**Take a magnet. And there are iron filings lying there. Put that magnet in among them — as many as can stick will stick and cling.**

**The negatively-charged particle: these come and sit on top of it, and then to the root — they are pulled. Because what is the root? Negative charge.**

> **Positive attracts to negative, negative attracts to positive. We have all played with magnets. Positive to positive does not `[attract]`, negative to negative does not.**

**We will look at cation exchange capacity in detail.** It is low in coarse soil, low in `[sandy]` soil, low in stony soil, **high in black soil, high in clay soil. And how to increase it, we will also have to look at.**

`[He closes with a partly-corrupted passage on salts: if nutrients and salts become excessive, the crop will not get them, and they will trouble the crop.]`

---

# Analysis — Part 8

## The strongest material

**The buffering-capacity section is the most valuable in this session, and it directly answers a question you will face at Pulivendula.**

His argument: acid injected into calcareous soil does not durably lower pH, because the carbonate system neutralises it; the reaction consumes the acid, produces reaction products, and the pH returns. **This is correct and it is the single most common misconception among farmers on calcareous ground.** It is why acid injection into a high-lime soil is a recurring cost with a transient effect.

**The EC / osmotic argument is also correct.** High EC raises the osmotic potential of the soil solution, so the plant must expend more energy to extract water — and this bites hardest in summer, exactly when demand peaks. The straw analogy conveys it well.

**The counter-intuitive nutrient point is right and under-appreciated:** low-EC irrigation water moves applied nutrients into the plant; high-EC water leaves them in the soil. The practical rule that follows — **higher water EC means you should apply less fertilizer, not more** — is the opposite of what most farmers do when they see deficiency symptoms.

**"Organic carbon is the soul of the soil"** and the emphasis on *conserving* it rather than only adding it is correct. Additions without protection burn off, especially at your temperatures.

**The CEC explanation via surface area and the magnet analogy** is accurate and well-taught. The listed relationships (clay high, sand low, organic matter raises it) are standard.

## Errors worth knowing about

| Claim | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **Copper becomes available at pH 7.5, alongside P and K** | **Wrong.** Copper availability, like zinc, iron and manganese, *increases* as pH falls. Grouping it with phosphorus and potassium misplaces it in the cascade. |
| **"pH does not affect molybdenum much"** | **Wrong, and backwards.** Molybdenum is the one micronutrient whose availability *increases* with rising pH. In your high-pH situation Mo is the least likely micronutrient to be deficient — the opposite of the others. |
| **"If biological and physical properties are good, chemical properties need no attention"** | **The most consequential overreach in the course.** In a calcareous soil, pH is buffered by the carbonate system at 8.0–8.5 and will not be moved to 6.5 by organic matter. Sodicity is a chemical problem requiring a chemical amendment. Organic matter improves nutrient *cycling* within the pH the carbonate sets — it does not reset the pH. **He contradicts himself two paragraphs later by saying Maharashtra's problem is high pH and must be actively managed.** |
| **640 TDS = 1 EC, and "350" for nitrate/chloride-rich water** | The 640 factor is standard for EC 0.1–5 dS/m. The second figure is garbled and does not match the usual direction — chloride-dominant waters typically run *at or slightly below* 640, not half of it. **Do not use the second number; measure EC directly.** |
| **Blood pH 7.38 and cancer** | The blood pH figure is right and the analogy is pedagogically fine. The causal link to diet and cancer is not — blood pH is tightly regulated and is not meaningfully shifted by food. |
| **"EC above 3 means stop fertilizer entirely"** | Directionally sound but too absolute. Above ~3 dS/m the response is leaching, drainage, and gypsum where sodicity is involved — not simply withholding nutrition, which would create the deficiency he warns about elsewhere. |

## Direct application to Pulivendula

This session is the most decision-relevant one yet for the 2027 planting.

1. **Expect pH 8.0–8.5 on calcareous ground, and plan around it rather than against it.** By his own table that leaves calcium, magnesium and sulphur freely available and puts **phosphorus, zinc, iron and manganese** at risk. That is the classic calcareous nutrition profile, and pomegranate on such soil typically shows iron and zinc chlorosis first.
2. **Do not budget for acid injection as a pH solution.** His buffering argument is correct: on high-lime soil it is a recurring cost with a transient effect. Localised acidification in the wetted bulb can help marginally; whole-soil pH change will not happen.
3. **Plan for chelated micronutrients rather than sulphate salts.** At pH 8+, ZnSO₄ and FeSO₄ applied to soil largely precipitate. **Fe-EDDHA** is the form that holds up above pH 7.5; foliar application is the other route.
4. **Measure irrigation water EC before design.** His 0.2–1.2 mS/cm target is a reasonable working range. If your borewell exceeds it — likely in that part of Kadapa district — the plantation needs a leaching fraction built into the irrigation schedule from day one, and the fertilizer programme must be scaled down, not up.
5. **Avoid chloride-carrying fertilizers** (MOP) where water EC is already elevated. Use SOP instead, accepting the cost difference.
6. **Treat organic carbon as the one lever that genuinely works on calcareous soil** — not because it fixes pH, but because it improves cycling, chelation and structure within the pH the carbonate dictates. That reframing is the honest version of his hierarchy claim.

---

# PART 9 — Liebig's Law of the Minimum (का घटतं उत्पादन?)

> Sources: PPT slide + audio transcript (moderately corrupted). ASR slips corrected inline: `गाळींब` = **डाळिंब** (pomegranate); `तेलकडा` = **तेलकट डाग** (bacterial blight / oily spot); `फुटबॉल` = **foot valve**; `तुडतुडा` = **jassid**; `शार` = **क्षार** (salts); `संजीवक` = **growth regulators / bio-stimulants**.

## 9.1 PPT Slide

### Slide — What is balanced nutrient management?

# Liebig's Law of the Minimum
## **का घटतं उत्पादन? — Why does yield decline?**

*Visual: the classic barrel with staves of unequal height. The water inside is labelled **YIELD**, and it spills out at the level of the shortest stave.*

**The staves shown:**

| | |
|---|---|
| Manganese | Molybdenum |
| Sodium | Copper |
| Water | Zinc |
| Soil conditions and other growth factors | Phosphorus |
| Light | Potassium |
| Sulphur | Magnesium |
| Calcium | Nitrogen |
| Boron | Iron |

> **The barrel holds only as much water as the shortest stave allows — no matter how tall the others are.**

---

## 9.2 Session Transcript

### Soil testing and the lab

**In this session we will look at** `[unclear]`. **If we want to check `[the soil]`, we will have to do soil testing. It must be done at a good lab. And I am not promoting any particular lab. My dream is that our Farm DSS should have its own lab.** But wherever you feel the lab is good, get your soil and water checked there.

**And then we will come to know:**

- Which nutrients are present in our soil
- Which nutrients are deficient
- **Which nutrients are present in excess of requirement**
- **And because of that excess, which nutrients will not become available**

**And then we have to take the decision on how to do nutrient management.** And in doing it — **with the minimum chemical and the maximum organic.**

### Know the requirement before you apply anything

**Before giving nutrients — before applying fertilizer in organic, chemical or biological form — we need to know: what is the nitrogen requirement of this crop of mine? What is the phosphorus requirement? The potash requirement? The boron requirement?**

**Because if I don't know what it needs, and at which stage it needs it —**

### Example 1: the potash–boron antagonism

> **Suppose potash is available in large quantity in my soil. And when potash becomes excessive, boron deficiency occurs — even if we apply it.**

**Now if I am at the flowering stage — if boron is needed for pollination, for increasing the length of the pollen tube — or if sugar loading is needed in my crop and boron is required for that.**

> **But if potash has become very high in my soil, that boron will not become available. Even if I give it.**

**So instead of giving it through the soil, can I give it through spraying?**

**To build our own recipe for that crop, we need to know: what is the requirement of that nutrient, at which stage, and in what quantity.**

---

### Example 2: The Nashik grape export case

**You will have heard in Nashik: *I want to export grapes.* And if you want to export grapes, the potash requirement stays high — because:**

- The size must be good
- The `[bloom/point]` must be good
- The sweetness must be good
- **The crunchiness must be there in that berry**
- **And the stem — if you hold the stem and lift the bunch like this, not a single berry should drop off**

**And all of these functions are performed by potash.**

**So the farmer was told only and only: apply potash, apply potash, apply potash.** And when the final stage came, `[the diagnosis was]` potash shortage. **Why? Because I want to export.** *Now I have no option but to give potash here.* **So some farmers were giving MOP, some farmers were giving SOP.**

**The soil test numbers:**

| Time point | Available potash |
|---|---|
| Year one of the vineyard (soil test ~10 years ago) | **300 / 350 / 400 kg per hectare** |
| Soil test 10 years later | **1,000 / 1,100 / 1,200 kg per hectare** |

> **That is how enormously we raised the potash there. Why? Because I want to export.**
>
> **Then tell me — did the export happen? No.**

**Because boron availability fell there. The incidence of short berry increased there. Sugar problems started to be created there.**

**We were told only one thing: if you want to increase sugar, you need potash. Give potash.**

> **But along with that potash, boron is also needed to increase sugar. Along with that potash, manganese is needed for disease control and to build disease resistance. Along with that potash, iron is needed to give the plant endurance. Along with that potash, zinc is needed to balance all the hormones in that plant.**
>
> **Nobody told us this. But we kept giving potash, giving potash, giving potash.**

---

### On the difficulty of technical sessions

**The technical session has now begun. And in a technical session you may at some point feel: do I really need all of these things?**

**The reason for that feeling: first, your education is not in soil science or agricultural chemistry. And our brain is not built to do so much hard grinding — it likes enjoyment. The moment you say you must learn something new, it immediately gets distressed.** *Hasn't farming been going on so far without all this?*

**But for the first three days I told you only one thing: there is no alternative to you. If any change is to be brought about in nutrient management in this society, it is in your hands.**

> **So I am going to teach you by going right down to the ground. I will give such fine-grained examples.**

**Anyone here who has studied agricultural chemistry or soil science or chemistry will think — why is he speaking so much on pH, is pH such a big thing to talk about? But I know that even my fourth- and fifth-standard farmer must understand those words, that terminology. And it is understood through examples.**

**So these videos will be continuously available to you. Whenever you get time — don't go to those other YouTube channels; go into the AgriAcademy application and put on this video.**

---

### His wish: the Padwa gathering

`[He discloses a personal aspiration, prefacing it by noting he is a three-time Landmark Forum graduate and has done the advanced training, and that the Forum teaches you to state your inner desires publicly.]`

> **We started on the day of Padwa — on our Hindu New Year. My wish, and I now see this dream: that next year, on the day before Padwa, on our farm, all of us — I expect some ten thousand farmers who have completed this course and who have grown their own food on their own farms — we should have a get-together of all these farmers.**
>
> **And on that day I am going to invite Sadhguru.** `[Reference to the Save Soil campaign and the 30,000 km journey across India.]` **I will go and hold his feet and say: Sadhguru, to walk on your path we have prepared ten thousand people. And we want to hear you alone. We want your feet to touch our farm.**

**And that get-together — only those who have done the training. Because we don't want negativity there. We don't want marketing there. We don't want products there. Whatever we have taken up, all of it we want to show there. Everything I do, you will get to see there.**

`[He notes practical limits: in 2018 a similar event drew 25,000–30,000 farmers, and food and water arrangements for that number are beyond one farmer. So it will be limited to course participants, opened up afterwards.]`

---

### Liebig's Law of the Minimum

**In today's session — Liebig's Law. There is a scientist, and that scientist first put forward his view, and after a great deal of controversy the whole world came around to him, and it is accepted that he is right.**

> **But the sad thing is that this law has still not been told to any of our farmers. It lies buried in books. Nobody unfolded it and explained it to our farmer.**
>
> **And when it is unfolded for you today, you will say exactly this: I would not have spent so much. I would not have spent so much.**

**If I had known that this thing was `[missing]` — only then would applying it have given a result. But this thing is not there at all, and however much I apply, the result will not come.**

### Two examples of futile expenditure

**Example A — bacterial blight in pomegranate:**

> **Suppose oily spot (bacterial blight) has come in the pomegranate, and fifty percent of the fruits have cracked. And then someone comes to the farm and advises: take this spray, it is a bit expensive, but your blight will be controlled.**
>
> **Fifty percent of fruits cracked means one hundred percent of the fruits are infected. Even if that product is meant to kill that bacterium — those are infected fruits, and mine are already gone. Which means one hundred percent of my fruits are going to crack and fall. So why should I spend?**

**Example B — nutrient spraying in a waterlogged field:**

> **A nutrient deficiency has occurred on a very large scale in my crop, and water is standing across my entire field. And to fill that deficiency, someone comes and tells me: walk through this mud and take a spray, your crop will be saved.**
>
> **I say: until your feeder root is activated, that nutrient is not going to go inside that leaf at all. Until there is sunlight there, it is not going to go inside.**
>
> **Continuous rain is falling, it has become mud below, water is standing — and we keep doing something or other.**

**These are the things we have to give up. And we have to do farming with less labour and less expense.**

---

### The barrel model

**What that scientist did:**

> **Picture a drum in front of you — the drum we keep, in which we specially put medicine into two hundred litres of water, or mix whatever nutrient there is, and give it to the crop. Like that drum.**
>
> **And in it you have made compartments, compartments, compartments — one compartment each for every one of the things that crop needs. And those compartments are joined together. And then you filled water into it.**
>
> **That water is your yield. That water is your yield.**

**Now this drum — if all the staves in it are at one level, then whatever water is filled will fill right to the top. Which means we get that big a yield from it.**

> **But if any one of these staves becomes short, then `[the water only fills]` up to that point** — as shown in the picture.

**So: whatever yield you want to take from that crop, all the things it needs for that yield `[must be present]`.**

---

### Scope discipline: stay in your lane

**Now, in this entire session that will run for a year, what are we going to look at? Only nutrient management. Only nutrient management.**

> **You must be equally expert in disease and pest management. You must be equally expert in weed management. You must be equally expert in agronomic practices.**

**`[Hearsay]` — I have been using this word from day one — this hearsay that is going on will not do.**

**So people think: you should have thirty crops, you should have forty crops. Is it possible to do a PhD in every crop?**

### The PhD critique

> **Because someone says *I have a PhD in pomegranate.* Do you know what his PhD is actually in? What changes occur in the pomegranate tree after removing water shoots. That is what the PhD is on — and for you that is a routine, ongoing job. You go and remove those water shoots. He did research on it. And so he says he has earned a doctorate in pomegranate. In which subject, though?**
>
> **Someone says *I have a PhD in entomology.* Take a crop — cotton. In that cotton there is the jassid, and for that jassid there are three insecticides, and how resistance develops to those insecticides — on that much of a subject he has done his PhD. He worked on it for three or four years. He got a doctorate in it. And he says he has done a PhD in cotton entomology.**

**And the farmer — from the foot valve, from the starter, from the `[pump]`, from the `[valve]`, from the filter, to the drip line; putting fertilizer into the soil, sowing the seed, selecting the variety, growing it, bringing it to flower, setting fruit, giving it nutrients, killing the diseases that come on it, killing the pests that come on it, harvesting, packing, grading, transporting, taking it to market, selling it, taking the money and repaying the dealer he bought the medicine from, paying the labour — and whatever money is left, keeping it in the family, building his house.**

> **What is it that we do not do? Think about it. So why do you underestimate yourself — saying no, he is a doctor, knowledge is in his hands?**
>
> **From that foot valve to building our house, we are all engineers. And has it not been proved by now? We run our family only on farming — we have proved it. Those people have not proved it. Sorry to say.**
>
> **Ten cows in our shed — and in some people's houses there is not even a cat, and they advise on how to keep cows. So I say: do not underestimate yourself.**

---

### Every crop is now off-season

**Take your mind away from those bags. First understand basic agriculture knowledge.**

**And then my thinking about that crop must be: in which conditions does this crop of mine come out excellently, give quality production, give higher production — that is, I get higher yield? Then only will I plant it, won't I?**

> **And if I want to go off-seasonal — now all crops have become off-seasonal. Listen carefully. Now all crops have become off-seasonal, because the season no longer exists.**

**Even today we are in the month of April, and in the morning it is cold, cold; in the afternoon it becomes tremendously hot. But in the morning it is cold. If you get up sometime at four, four-thirty, five and go outside, it is properly cold.** `[So]` **the season no longer remains. So we will have to call all crops off-seasonal.**

**The grape example:**

> **Earlier we used to say: once the grape crop is `[past a stage]`, once your `[flowering]` is done, once your berries have set — then you need not fear downy mildew at all. After that downy mildew simply does not come.**
>
> **And now? Now downy mildew sprays are being taken at the harvesting stage.**

**Because at that time, once thinning and so on was done in the winter days and everything was set, after that rain simply did not come. No rain means no humidity, leaves not wet — no humidity means downy mildew will not grow. So downy mildew did not come.**

> **But now that condition no longer remains. Sometimes rain came, sometimes the leaves were wet, sometimes there was dew.**

**So if someone now says your produce should come off-seasonal so you get good rates — you certainly do get good rates. But now the season itself no longer exists. Whenever you take a crop, it is off-seasonal.**

> **Which means we will have to study more, take more technical knowledge, and implement that technical knowledge. So do not become an expert in too many crops. We don't even have that capacity — because we have to do the work from the foot valve to the market, and up to the investment of the money we bring in.**

---

### The definition of nutrient management

> **Taking responsibility for carrying it to that crop and sending it from the soil right up to the top — that whole thing is nutrient management.**
>
> **Making available the nutrient my crop needs, in the quantity it needs, in the form it needs, at the stage it needs — that responsibility is B. T. Gore's. Instead of B. T. Gore, you have to put your own name.**

**If you have only brought the bags, mixed all the bags together, mixed them all into the soil, given water, and if you then say that all the nutrients I gave went into my crop — then you are asleep. You simply don't know. You simply don't know.**

### Indiscriminate and injudicious use

> **And this is called *indiscriminate and injudicious use* — अवाजवी वापर. Indiscriminate and injudicious means using that nutrient excessively and even when there is no need.**

**And has our problem started from here? Because bags mean trade. Bags mean trade.**

> **Trade means: this year ten thousand bags sold; next year twelve thousand; the year after fifteen thousand; the year after twenty thousand. Trade arrived. And then — not one bag. Two bags. And two bags came. How much was the requirement? Nobody knows. Nobody knows.**

**And then what happened — whatever new fertilizers came to the market, `[they were sold on the claim that]` strength comes, strength has gone.**

**But this was never looked at: what lies behind it?**

- **Which nutrient is deficient, which nutrient is in excess**
- **Which one suppresses which**
- **Which one helps another to be taken up**
- **Which fertilizer works with which fertilizer, and which does not**
- **What chemical reaction occurs**
- **How acid gets formed once it goes there**
- **How precipitation occurs once it goes there**
- **How the soil micro-organisms are affected**

> **A hundred percent blind eye was turned to this. Because the people who commented on nutrient management all had a product. And if they had come and said good, honest things like B. T. Gore, their business would not have happened, their trade would not have happened.**

---

### Pomegranate nutrient removal — the numbers

> **This black soil, these rocks — in these rocks it is present in such enormous quantity that it can never be exhausted. It can never be exhausted.**

**To produce one tonne of pomegranate:**

| Rank | Nutrient | Requirement per tonne of fruit |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Potash** | **3 kg 70 g** |
| 2 | **Nitrogen** | **2 kg 270 g** |
| 3 | **Calcium** | **920 g** |
| 4 | **Phosphorus** | **460 g** |

> **That is the situation. And how much are we applying? How much is falling into that soil? How much is `[running off]` with the water? How much is our groundwater being spoiled?**

**And then that same water is what we have to drink, so we install `[an RO unit]`, and take all of it out, and drink low-salt water. Which means we have shut off even the salts we needed — because the salts in our water became too high.**

### The question to ask any salesman

> **So I say: now at least, when someone comes and starts telling you *use this product, you will get tremendous productivity* — ask him only one question:**
>
> ***When did your product arrive? Just now, isn't it? I have been getting yield from my crop without your product too. And I get yield with your product too. So what is it to me?***

**If our grandfathers, if our fathers had asked this question, then perhaps B. T. Gore would not have needed to shout himself hoarse now. There would not have been this need to play with your psychology and your emotions.**

`[He acknowledges the objection:]` **Now if people say you are speaking against the market, you are reducing people's business — then yes, I am. I am.**

### On growth regulators (संजीवक)

**Because I tell you: you are going to apply growth regulators again — and on growth regulators I will take not one but two sessions in detail. Because I have my complete book, and my own study of it is now under way. I will unpack that whole book of physiology for you.**

**And in it, how all the growth regulators work, which crop works how.**

> **Because these growth regulators are `[not something you need to supply]` — the plant produces them itself. But if you are applying them from above, then some components get formed that create conditions for disease to come into that plant, for pests to come, and that are not good for us either — and because of them we fall ill.**

### What your mindset should be

> **So what should our mindset be? *Until now I was using this. I do not need it. I do not need it. I will do nutrient management well, won't I? I will feed it properly, won't I?***
>
> **So what does this scientist say? See whether all the nutrients are correct.**

---

# Analysis — Part 9

## The strongest session in the course

Part 9 does something the earlier sessions kept gesturing at: it gives a **mechanism for why a single-nutrient strategy fails**, and backs it with a documented case and hard numbers.

**Liebig's Law is correctly stated and correctly applied.** The barrel is the standard teaching image, and his framing is right: yield is set by the most limiting factor, so adding more of an already-sufficient nutrient produces nothing. The slide correctly includes non-nutrient staves — water, light, soil conditions — which is the version of Liebig that actually matters in the field.

**The Nashik grape case is the best evidence he has presented.** Available potash rising from 300–400 kg/ha to 1,000–1,200 kg/ha over a decade, with the export objective still unmet, is a concrete and checkable claim. **The potassium–boron antagonism he describes is real** — high K depresses B uptake, and boron is genuinely required for pollen tube elongation and for sugar translocation. So is his broader point that potassium interacts antagonistically with magnesium and calcium. This is the single most useful diagnostic idea in the course: **an excess is a deficiency in disguise.**

**"Fifty percent cracked means one hundred percent infected"** is sharp economic reasoning. Once the pathogen is systemic and the crop is committed, further spend is sunk cost. Applying this test before authorising any rescue spray would save most farmers real money.

**The waterlogging point is physiologically correct.** Foliar uptake needs an active transpiration stream and light-driven metabolism; spraying into a saturated, overcast field wastes product. This follows directly from his Part 4 transpiration framework — the course is now internally consistent in a way it was not earlier.

**The scope discipline is honest and unusual.** Explicitly saying "this course covers only nutrition, and you need equal competence in pest, disease, weed and agronomy" is the opposite of the usual claim that one framework solves everything.

## Cautions

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **"Nutrients in the rock can never be exhausted"** | Repeated from Part 4 and still overstated. Total reserves are large; **plant-available** fractions are the constraint, and the pomegranate removal figures he gives on the same page are precisely a measure of what leaves the system each year. He is arguing both sides. |
| **The removal figures (K 3.07, N 2.27, Ca 0.92, P 0.46 kg/t)** | Plausible for fruit removal, but **fruit removal is not the full crop requirement.** Leaves, wood, root turnover and pruning losses all draw nutrients. Using removal figures alone to set a fertiliser budget will under-fertilise a young, expanding orchard. Treat them as a floor, not a recommendation. |
| **The PhD critique** | The observation that specialisation is narrow is fair. The dismissal is not — narrow research is how the resistance data and the water-shoot physiology he himself relies on get generated. His better point is the one about farmer competence, which stands on its own without the swipe. |
| **"Growth regulators — the plant makes them itself, don't apply them"** | Overstated as a blanket rule. Some PGR applications are well-established and safe within label rates. His caution about routine, unnecessary use is sound; the categorical version is not. |
| **Landmark Forum and the Sadhguru invitation** | Note for context: the session includes a personal aspiration and an institutional endorsement. Neither affects the technical content, but it is worth registering that the course carries a movement-building agenda alongside the agronomy. |
