# PART 13 — Organic Carbon Management — PART Q: VERMICOMPOST (Method 4 of 10)

> Sources: two PPT slides (materials list, and the full 11-step bed-method protocol). ASR/reading notes: `आयसेनिया फेटीडा` = ***Eisenia fetida***; `गांडूळ बीज / गांडूळ कल्चर` = **earthworm seed / culture**; `बारदान` = **gunny sack (jute)**; `भुसभुशीत` = **friable/crumbly**; `रवाळ` = **granular**; `लाल मुंग्या` = **red ants**.

## 13Q.0 The materials

The first slide gives just three inputs — vermicomposting is the simplest of the composting methods in terms of ingredients:

| # | Material (Marathi) | English |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **वाळलेला पालापाचोळा** | **Dried leaf litter** |
| 2 | **शेणखत (अर्धवट कुजलेले)** | **FYM — SEMI-decomposed** |
| 3 | **गांडूळ बीज** | **Earthworm seed / culture** |

> ⚡ **NOTE THE DELIBERATE EXCEPTION —** everywhere else in this course Gore insists on **fully decomposed** FYM (Part 13J's entire warning list). Here he specifies **अर्धवट कुजलेले — semi-decomposed.** This is correct and intentional: **the worms need partially-broken-down material to feed on.** Fully composted material has already had its readily-available carbon consumed, and fresh raw manure generates heat that kills worms. Semi-decomposed is the correct feedstock window for vermicomposting specifically.

---

## 13Q.1 The species

> **गांडूळखत निर्मितीसाठी गांडुळाची आयसेनिया फेटीडा ही प्रजात वापरावी**
>
> ⚡ **SPECIES —** **use *Eisenia fetida*** (the red wiggler / tiger worm) for vermicompost production.

This is the correct and standard choice. *Eisenia fetida* is an **epigeic** species — it lives in surface organic matter rather than burrowing into mineral soil, tolerates high organic loads and a wide temperature range, and reproduces rapidly. It is the global standard for vermicomposting. (Deep-burrowing *anecic* species like *Lampito mauritii* are soil-improvers but poor composters.)

---

## 13Q.2 The bed method — the complete 11-step protocol

The slide notes: *"There are various methods for making vermicompost; of these, the **bed method** is the most convenient."*

### Bed specification

| Element | Specification |
|---|---|
| **Bed type** | **Plastic bed** (the commercial vermibed) |
| **Dimensions** | **12 ft × 4 ft × 2 ft** |
| **Charge per bed** | **1,500 kg** of material |
| **Worm culture** | **5 kg per bed** |
| **Output** | **1 tonne of vermicompost per bed, in 2½ months** |

### The full sequence

| Step | Instruction |
|---|---|
| **1** | To make vermicompost by the bed method, **use a plastic bed**; the bed is generally of the size **12 × 4 × 2 ft** |
| **2** | For vermicompost production, use the earthworm species ***Eisenia fetida*** |
| **3** | First, put **1,500 kg of dried leaf litter and semi-decomposed FYM into the bed in ALTERNATING LAYERS**, and **sprinkle water for four consecutive days** |
| **4** | **On the fourth day, release 5 kg of earthworm culture per bed** |
| **5** | **Then cover the bed properly with WET JUTE GUNNY SACKS** |
| **6** | The composting process now begins |
| **7** | **For the next 2½ months, keep sprinkling enough water that the gunny sacking does not dry out** |
| **8** | **In 2 to 2½ months, granular, tea-powder-like, dark-coloured, friable compost is ready** |
| **9** | **Then stop sprinkling water.** Once the topmost layer of the compost dries, **remove it**. Then remove the second layer in the same way after it dries, **and finally collect the earthworms at the bottom** |
| **10** | **Harvesting this way makes it easy to separate the earthworms from the compost** |
| **11** | **Each bed produces 1 tonne of compost in 2½ months** |

### The critical warning

> ⚡ **महत्त्वाचे — IMPORTANT: "बेडचे लाल मुंग्यापासून संरक्षण करावे" — PROTECT THE BED FROM RED ANTS.**

Red ants are the primary predator of vermicompost beds. They will carry off worms and eggs and can destroy a bed entirely. Standard countermeasures are a water channel around the bed legs, or ash/neem barriers.

---

## 13Q.3 The harvesting logic — why the drying method matters

Step 9 is the cleverest part of the protocol and deserves explanation, because it solves the main practical problem of vermicomposting: **separating worms from finished compost.**

The method exploits worm behaviour:

1. **Stop watering.** The bed begins to dry from the top down.
2. **Worms move away from dry conditions** — they migrate downward into the still-moist lower layers.
3. **Skim off the dried top layer** — it is nearly worm-free.
4. **Let the next layer dry, remove it** — repeat.
5. **The worms concentrate at the bottom** and are collected as a dense population, ready to seed the next bed.

> ⚡ **THE PRINCIPLE —** *don't sieve worms out of compost; let the worms sort themselves out by following the moisture down.* This is why the slide says harvesting this way "makes it easy to separate the earthworms from the compost."

---

## 13Q.4 Vermicompost in context — what the earlier parts already told us

Cross-referencing the composition table from Part 13I:

| Vermicompost | Value |
|---|---|
| **Organic carbon** | **16–23%** |
| **Nitrogen** | 1.0% |
| **Phosphorus (P₂O₅)** | 1.0% |
| **Potash (K₂O)** | 1.0% |

Compare with FYM (15–20% OC, 0.5-0.25-0.5 NPK): **vermicompost carries roughly double the nutrients of FYM at similar carbon content**, plus a far higher microbial and humic load.

It also appears twice more in the course:

- **Part 13K (Organic Culture):** vermicompost is one of the two carriers (150 kg) for the 11-ingredient microbial consortium — chosen because it is "microbially-rich, humus-bearing"
- **Part 2:** 50 kg vermicompost is in the base dressing per 5 guntha, described as "a concentrated inoculant of beneficial microbes and humic substances"

> ⚡ **ITS ROLE —** vermicompost is not primarily a bulk carbon source (FYM and press mud are cheaper per tonne for that). Its value is as a **concentrated microbial and humic inoculant** — a small quantity of high-quality biological material.

---

# Analysis — Part 13Q

## A clean, correct, standard protocol

This is textbook vermicomposting, correctly specified. Every element checks out: *Eisenia fetida* is the right species, semi-decomposed feedstock is the right condition, 12×4×2 ft plastic beds are the standard commercial format, wet jute covering maintains the darkness and humidity worms need, and 2–2½ months is a realistic cycle time.

**The semi-decomposed FYM specification is the technically important detail**, and it is easy to get wrong precisely because the rest of the course hammers "fully decomposed." The reasoning is sound: fresh manure heats during active decomposition and will kill worms; fully-finished compost has little left for them to eat. The window in between is where vermicomposting works.

**The dry-down harvesting method is genuinely elegant** and is the standard professional technique — it exploits the worms' hydrotaxis rather than fighting it with sieves, and it preserves the breeding population intact for the next cycle.

**The red-ant warning is correct and often underestimated.** Ant predation is one of the most common causes of vermibed failure in India.

## Practical numbers

| Metric | Value |
|---|---|
| Input | 1,500 kg per bed |
| Output | **1,000 kg (1 tonne) per bed** |
| Conversion | **~67%** by weight |
| Cycle | 2½ months |
| Worm stocking | 5 kg per bed |
| Beds needed for 1 tonne/cycle | 1 |

> **Realistic throughput:** one bed produces ~1 t per 2½ months, so **roughly 4 tonnes per bed per year** running continuously.

## Cautions and additions

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **Temperature** | *Eisenia fetida* performs best around **20–30 °C** and suffers above ~35 °C. **In Pulivendula this is the binding constraint** — beds must be in deep shade, and in peak summer you may need additional cooling (extra watering, shade net, or siting under trees). The slide does not mention this and it matters more for you than for Maharashtra. |
| **Moisture** | The wet-gunny instruction handles it, but the target is roughly 60–70% moisture — damp like a wrung-out sponge, never waterlogged. Waterlogging kills worms. |
| **No chemicals, ever** | Worms are far more sensitive than microbes. Any pesticide, fungicide or fertilizer residue in the feedstock or water will kill the population. Feedstock provenance matters. |
| **Other predators** | Beyond red ants: rats, birds, frogs, and centipedes. A mesh cover plus the water-moat legs handles most. |
| **Avoid** in the feedstock | Citrus peel in quantity (acidic), salt, oils, and anything from a chemically-treated crop. |
| **The species name** | Note that *Eisenia fetida* is often confused with *Eudrilus eugeniae* (African nightcrawler), also widely sold in India. *E. eugeniae* is faster in warm climates but more heat- and handling-sensitive. For Pulivendula's heat, *E. eugeniae* is sometimes preferred locally — worth asking your supplier what they actually stock. |

## For Pulivendula

1. **Heat is your main obstacle, and the slide doesn't address it.** Site the beds in **deep shade** — under trees, or in a shed with shade net. Monitor bed temperature in April–June; above ~35 °C the worms will stop feeding, and above ~38 °C they will die. This single factor will determine whether vermicomposting works on your farm.

2. **Vermicompost is a quality input, not a bulk one — size accordingly.** At ~4 t/bed/year, you would need many beds to supply a 4-acre block's bulk carbon needs (recall the Gayatri Sanjeevani recipe alone calls for 10 t FYM *per acre*). **Use FYM, press mud and green manure for bulk; use vermicompost as the concentrated microbial/humic component** — in the planting pits, in the organic-culture carrier (Part 13K), and as a starter.

3. **Two or three beds is a sensible starting scale.** That gives 8–12 t/year — enough for planting-pit charging across 2,000 trees, and enough to supply the 150 kg carrier for organic-culture batches, without over-committing labour.

4. **Ask your supplier which species they actually stock.** *E. fetida* is what the slide specifies; *E. eugeniae* is common in south India and handles heat somewhat better. Either works; know which you have, because their temperature tolerances differ.

5. **Feedstock discipline.** Your prunings and cover-crop residue are ideal, but they must be **semi-decomposed first** — pre-compost them for a few weeks before they go into the vermibed. And nothing from a sprayed crop.

6. **Protect against ants from day one**, not after you lose a bed. Water moats under the bed legs are the cheapest reliable method.

---

## Addendum to the running Interaction-Rules master list (§13C.5)

Add these from Part 13Q:

> ⚡ **Vermicompost uses SEMI-decomposed FYM, not fully decomposed** — the deliberate exception to the course's usual rule. Fresh manure heats and kills worms; finished compost has nothing left to eat.
> ⚡ **Species: *Eisenia fetida*** (epigeic, surface-dwelling). Bed **12 × 4 × 2 ft**, charge **1,500 kg**, culture **5 kg/bed**, output **1 tonne in 2½ months** (~67% conversion).
> ⚡ **Sequence:** alternating layers of dried leaf litter + semi-decomposed FYM → water 4 days → release worms on day 4 → cover with **wet jute gunny** → keep gunny damp for 2½ months.
> ⚡ **Harvest by dry-down:** stop watering, worms migrate downward away from dryness, skim dried layers off the top, collect concentrated worms at the bottom. Never sieve.
> ⚡ **PROTECT BEDS FROM RED ANTS** — the primary predator and a common cause of total bed failure.
> ⚡ **Heat limit (not in slide):** *E. fetida* suffers above ~35 °C. In hot regions beds must be in deep shade — the binding constraint on vermicomposting in semi-arid areas.

---

*Translated through Part 13Q. Part R follows below — Method 6 of the ten: **Green Manure**, with the full species comparison table and the two green-manuring systems.*

---

# PART 13 — Organic Carbon Management — PART R: GREEN MANURE (Method 6 of 10)

> Sources: seven PPT slides (definition and the two systems; in-situ species; leaf-manure species; the full species comparison table with nitrogen contributions; selection and timing; what to avoid; and the benefits list). Reading notes: `हिरवळीचे खत` = **green manure**; `ताग` = **sunn hemp (*Crotalaria juncea*)**; `धैंचा` = **dhaincha (*Sesbania* spp.)**; `चवळी` = **cowpea**; `मूग` = **green gram/moong**; `उडीद` = **black gram/urad**; `मटकी` = **moth bean**; `गवार` = **cluster bean/guar**; `बरसीम` = **berseem clover**; `शेवरी` = **Sesbania sesban**; `गिरिपुष्प` = **Gliricidia sepium**; `सुबाभूळ` = **subabul (*Leucaena leucocephala*)**; `करंज` = **karanj (*Pongamia pinnata*)**; `कुळीथ` = **horse gram**; `मृगाचा पाऊस` = **the Mrig nakshatra rain (onset of monsoon)**.

## 13R.0 The definition and the two systems

> **या पिकाची लागवड विशेषतः हिरवी असतानाच जमिनीत गाडण्यासाठी केली जाते.**
>
> *This crop is grown specifically to be buried in the soil **while still green**.*

> **हिरवळीचे खत हे जमिनीतील सेंद्रिय पदार्थ, मातीची रचना, पुढील पिकासाठी नायट्रोजन आणि इतर पोषक तत्त्वांचा पुरवठा होण्यासाठी घेतले जाते.**
>
> *Green manure is taken to supply **soil organic matter, soil structure, and nitrogen and other nutrients for the following crop.***

### The two methods of burying green manure

| | **System 1 — In-situ** (त्याच जमिनीत उगवून, तेथेच गाडले जाते) | **System 2 — Leaf manure / ex-situ** (झाडांची कोवळी पाने आणि फांद्या बाहेरून आणून जमिनीत गाडली जातात) |
|---|---|---|
| **Method** | The crop is **grown in the same field and buried there** | **Tender leaves and branches are brought from elsewhere** and buried in the field |
| **Examples** | **ताग (sunn hemp), धैंचा (dhaincha)**, etc. | **गिरिपुष्प (Gliricidia), करंज (karanj), शेवरी (Sesbania sesban), सुबाभूळ (subabul)** |

---

## 13R.1 System 1 — In-situ green manure species

The slide shows ten species with photographs, plus a further list in text:

**Pictured:** ताग (sunn hemp) · धैंचा (dhaincha) · बरसीम (berseem) · मोहरी (mustard) · मूग (green gram) · चवळी (cowpea) · उडीद (black gram) · गवार (cluster bean) · कुळीथ (horse gram) · सोयाबीन (soybean)

**Also listed:** मेथी (fenugreek), लुपिन (lupin), वेच (vetch), बकव्हीट (buckwheat), किरमिजी रंगाचा क्लोव्हर (crimson clover), गोड क्लोव्हर (sweet clover), पर्शियन क्लोव्हर (Persian clover)

> **Note the overlap with your Andhra natural-farming mix from Part 13C** — cowpea, green gram, black gram, horse gram, cluster bean, soybean, fenugreek and mustard all appear in both lists. The two approaches converge.

---

## 13R.2 System 2 — Leaf manure (the border-tree method)

This is the more elegant system and the slide explains why:

> **शेतात बांधावर किंवा पडीक जागेवर गिरिपुष्प, शेवरी, करंज, सुबाभूळ इत्यादी पिकांची लागवड करून त्यांची कोवळी पाने अथवा कोवळ्या फांद्या तोडून जमिनीत गाडावीत.**
>
> *Plant Gliricidia, Sesbania sesban, karanj, subabul etc. **on the field bunds or on waste land**, then cut their tender leaves or tender branches and bury them in the soil.*

### The decisive advantage

> ⚡ **THE KEY BENEFIT —** *"या हिरवळीच्या पिकांचा एक मुख्य फायदा म्हणजे या पिकांसाठी ज्या जमिनीत मुख्य पीक घ्यावयाचे आहे तेथे हिरवळीच्या पिकाच्या लागवडीसाठी वाट बघावी लागत नाही."*
>
> **You do not have to wait — or give up a season — on the land where your main crop grows.** The green-manure plants live permanently on the bunds and waste ground; you simply harvest their leaves and branches and bury them in the cropped field.
>
> **त्यामुळे हंगाम वाया जाण्याची शक्यता नसते — therefore there is no possibility of losing a season.**

This directly solves the biggest practical objection to green manuring: that it costs you a cropping season.

---

## 13R.3 THE SPECIES TABLE — biomass, nitrogen % and nitrogen contributed

This is the most decision-useful table in the session. Note the last column is **kg of nitrogen released into the soil per ACRE**.

| # | Crop | Season | Green biomass (t/ha) | N content (%) | **N released into soil (kg/acre)** |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **ताग — Sunn hemp** | Kharif | **8.50** | 0.43 | **36.55** |
| 2 | **धैंचा — Dhaincha** | Kharif, Rabi | 7.50 | 0.42 | **31.50** |
| 3 | **चवळी — Cowpea** | Kharif, Rabi | 6.00 | 0.49 | **44.10** |
| 4 | **मूग — Green gram** | Kharif, Summer | 3.00 | 0.53 | 15.90 |
| 5 | **उडीद — Black gram** | Kharif | 3.50 | 0.85 | 29.75 |
| 6 | **मटकी — Moth bean** | Kharif, Rabi | 3.50 | 0.85 | 29.75 |
| 7 | **गवार — Cluster bean** | Kharif, Rabi | 6.00 | 0.34 | 27.20 |
| 8 | **बरसीम — Berseem** | Rabi | 4.00 | 0.43 | 17.20 |
| 9 | **शेवरी — Sesbania sesban** | **Perennial** | 7.50 | **2.43** | **182.25** |
| 10 | **गिरिपुष्प — Gliricidia** | **Perennial** | **25 kg/tree** | **2.53** | **630 g/tree** |
| 11 | **सुबाभूळ — Subabul** | **Perennial** | **20 kg/tree** | **3.20** | **640 g/tree** |

### How to read this table

> ⚡ **THE STANDOUT —** **शेवरी (Sesbania sesban) at 182.25 kg N/acre is in a different league entirely** — five times sunn hemp, four times cowpea. Its nitrogen *content* (2.43%) is roughly five times that of the annual legumes, and it produces 7.5 t/ha of biomass. **This is the single highest nitrogen contributor on the list.**

> ⚡ **THE PER-TREE OPTIONS —** Gliricidia at **630 g N/tree** and subabul at **640 g N/tree** are the leaf-manure workhorses. Their value is that they are *perennial* — planted once on bunds, harvested repeatedly, and they never cost you a cropping season.
>
> **The arithmetic:** at 630–640 g N per tree, **roughly 50 Gliricidia or subabul trees on your bunds deliver ~32 kg N per harvest** — comparable to a full acre of sunn hemp, but without using any cropped land.

> ⚡ **THE ANNUAL CHOICE —** among the in-situ annuals, **cowpea (44.10 kg N/acre) actually beats sunn hemp (36.55)** despite lower biomass, because of its higher nitrogen percentage. Cowpea is also more heat- and drought-tolerant, which matters for a semi-arid site.

---

## 13R.4 Selection and timing (निवड व काळजी)

### How to choose the crop

| Factor | Guidance |
|---|---|
| **पिकांची निवड — Crop selection** | Choose **leguminous (शेंगवर्गीय) crops**, taking into account **soil moisture** and the **time available for the manuring** |
| **पेरणीची वेळ — Sowing time** | **After the first Mrig-nakshatra rain** is considered the right time for sowing green-manure crops. However, this time can differ from place to place. **There must be sufficient moisture for germination** |
| **जमिनीमध्ये गाडण्याची योग्य वेळ — Right time to bury** | **Generally, bury it once the crop comes into flower. This takes approximately 6 to 8 weeks after sowing** |
| **Interval before sowing the main crop** | Plan the main crop's sowing according to **how long the buried crop takes to decompose. Thicker, succulent stems and leaves take less time to decompose** |

> ⚡ **THE TIMING RULE — bury at flowering, roughly 6–8 weeks after sowing.** *(Compare Part 2, where the instruction was "at pod initiation." Flowering is slightly earlier than pod initiation; both fall in the same window — after maximum biomass accumulation but before the plant lignifies and sets seed. Flowering is the safer instruction because it guarantees no seed set.)*

---

## 13R.5 What to AVOID — the seven cautions

This slide is unusually honest about green manuring's failure modes:

| # | Caution |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Taking green manure alone can waste an entire cropping season.** As far as possible, take the green-manure crop **as an INTERCROP** |
| 2 | **In rainfed (जिरायती) farming, low rainfall means green manure does not decompose properly.** As a result, it can affect the **germination and growth of the main crop** |
| 3 | **The green-manure crop must be buried before fruiting.** If delayed, **the carbon:nitrogen ratio of the crop rises. Fibrous material increases, and therefore the decomposition process slows down** |
| 4 | **In low-rainfall areas, taking a green-manure crop can reduce the soil moisture available to the main crop** |
| 5 | **Sunn hemp (ताग) is an excellent green manure but should only be taken where there is adequate rainfall or irrigation.** Although it grows well in all soil types, **this crop does not grow vigorously in ACIDIC soils** |
| 6 | **Continuous cultivation of green-manure crops can increase disease, pests and NEMATODES on the crop** |
| 7 | *(implied throughout)* Match the species to your moisture and season |

> ⚡ **THE MOISTURE WARNING IS THE MOST IMPORTANT FOR SEMI-ARID SITES —** cautions 2 and 4 together say: **in a low-rainfall area, a green-manure crop competes for the very moisture your main crop needs, AND may not decompose properly once buried.** This is a real constraint, not a footnote.

> ⚡ **THE NEMATODE WARNING (caution 6)** is significant and rarely mentioned — continuous green manuring with the same species can build up nematode populations. **Rotate green-manure species**, don't grow the same one every year.

---

## 13R.6 The benefits (हिरवळीच्या खताचे फायदे)

| # | Benefit |
|---|---|
| 1 | **Increases the quantity of organic matter in the soil** |
| 2 | **Changes the physical, chemical and biological properties of the soil** |
| 3 | **Because organic matter increases, soil microorganisms multiply** |
| 4 | **Increases the soil's water-holding capacity** |
| 5 | **Reduces soil erosion** |
| 6 | **Increases the availability of phosphorus, potash, calcium, manganese, iron etc.** |
| 7 | **Increases nitrogen in the soil; that nitrogen becomes available to the following crop** |
| 8 | **Green-manure crops take nutrients from the LOWER soil layers, and after burial those nutrients become available to the crop** |
| 9 | **Helps improve SALINE (क्षारयुक्त) soils** |
| 10 | **Helps to check NEMATODES** |
| 11 | **Taking green-manure crops reduces the spread and growth of weeds** |

> ⚡ **BENEFIT 8 — THE NUTRIENT PUMP.** *Green-manure crops draw nutrients from soil layers **below** the main crop's root zone, and burial brings those nutrients up into the surface layer where the main crop can reach them.* This is a genuinely valuable mechanism that most farmers never consider — deep-rooted green manures are effectively **mining and recycling subsoil nutrients**.

> ⚡ **BENEFITS 6, 9 AND 10 ARE THE THREE THAT MATTER MOST ON CALCAREOUS SOIL** — increased phosphorus/iron/manganese availability (exactly your locked nutrients), saline-soil improvement, and nematode suppression.

> ⚡ **NOTE THE APPARENT CONTRADICTION** — benefit 10 says green manure *checks* nematodes; caution 6 says continuous green manuring *increases* nematodes. **Both are true:** the right species (especially sunn hemp, which is genuinely nematode-suppressive) reduces nematodes, but repeating the *same* species indefinitely allows adapted populations to build. **The resolution is rotation of green-manure species.**

---

# Analysis — Part 13R

## The most decision-useful data in the carbon block

The species table is the standout. It is the first time the course has given **quantified nitrogen contributions per acre by species**, and it turns green manuring from a general recommendation into a selection decision.

**The three genuinely important findings in that table:**

1. **Sesbania sesban (शेवरी) at 182 kg N/acre** dwarfs everything else — 5× sunn hemp. Its 2.43% N content is the reason. As a perennial it also fits the leaf-manure system.
2. **Cowpea beats sunn hemp** (44 vs 36.5 kg N/acre) despite lower biomass, because of higher N%. And cowpea is more drought-tolerant — a better fit for semi-arid conditions.
3. **The perennial tree species (Gliricidia 630 g N/tree, subabul 640 g N/tree)** deliver nitrogen without occupying cropped land at all.

**The leaf-manure system (System 2) is the most practically important idea in the session**, and it dissolves the standard objection to green manuring. By growing nitrogen-fixing trees on bunds and waste ground and carrying their prunings into the field, you get the nitrogen and organic matter **without sacrificing a cropping season**. For a perennial orchard — where you cannot take the block out of production for a green-manure crop once trees are established — this is *the* applicable system.

**The honesty about failure modes is notable.** Most green-manure advocacy omits the moisture competition and the nematode build-up. Both cautions are real and correctly stated.

## Cautions and corrections

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **The N figures are estimates** | Biomass and N content both vary widely with soil, season, moisture and management. Treat the table as **relative ranking**, not as guaranteed values. The 182 kg/acre for Sesbania in particular assumes good growth. |
| **Unit inconsistency** | Biomass is given in t/**ha** while nitrogen is in kg/**acre**. This is how the slide presents it; be careful not to mix units when calculating. |
| **"Bury at flowering" vs "at pod initiation"** | Part 2 said pod initiation, this slide says flowering. Both are within the correct window; **flowering is the safer instruction** because it guarantees no viable seed is returned. On a semi-arid site, err toward flowering to conserve moisture. |
| **Sunn hemp in acidic soils** | The caution says sunn hemp grows poorly in acidic soil. **Your soil is the opposite — calcareous/alkaline — so this is not a constraint for you.** |
| **Subabul (*Leucaena*) caution not stated** | Subabul contains **mimosine**, which is toxic to livestock in quantity, and the species is invasive in some regions. Fine as a leaf-manure source; do not feed heavily to cattle, and manage seed spread. |
| **Gliricidia caution not stated** | *Gliricidia sepium* leaves and seeds are **toxic to livestock and to dogs**. Excellent leaf manure; keep animals away from prunings. |
| **Nematode suppression is species-specific** | Sunn hemp (*Crotalaria juncea*) is genuinely and well-evidenced as nematode-suppressive. Not all green manures are — some legumes are good nematode *hosts*. Choose deliberately. |

## For Pulivendula — this is your pre-planting and permanent programme

**Phase 1 — Before planting (2026–27): in-situ green manure across the block**

1. **Use a mix, not a monoculture** — combining your Andhra diverse-mix approach (Part 13C) with this table. **Cowpea and sunn hemp** as the backbone (highest N of the annuals, and sunn hemp for nematode suppression), plus horse gram and cluster bean which suit low moisture.
2. **Sow on the monsoon onset**, bury **at flowering, 6–8 weeks later.**
3. **Watch the moisture caution.** On a semi-arid site, the green-manure crop competes for moisture and may not decompose if the season is dry. Sow with the monsoon so both growth and decomposition have moisture.
4. **Rotate species between years** to avoid the nematode build-up (caution 6).

**Phase 2 — Permanent: plant the leaf-manure trees on your bunds NOW**

5. **This is the highest-value action from this session.** Plant **Gliricidia and Sesbania sesban (शेवरी) along the bunds and on any waste ground**, starting immediately. They take time to establish, so planting them in 2026 means they are productive by the time your Bhagwa is in the ground.
6. **The arithmetic works:** ~50 Gliricidia trees on bunds ≈ 32 kg N per harvest, repeatable several times a year, **without using a square metre of orchard land.** Sesbania sesban's very high N content makes it worth including too.
7. **This solves the perennial-orchard problem.** Once your trees are planted you cannot take the block out for green manure. Bund-grown leaf manure is the only green-manuring system that works permanently in an established orchard — cut prunings, spread or bury them in the inter-row.
8. **Keep livestock away from Gliricidia and subabul prunings** — both are toxic to animals.

**Why this matters especially for your soil:** benefits 6, 9 and 10 map precisely onto your constraints — green manure increases availability of **phosphorus, iron and manganese** (your pH-locked nutrients), helps improve **saline soils** (your EC problem), and **checks nematodes** (your Bhagwa root risk). Of all ten methods in this block, green manure addresses the most of your specific problems at once.

---

## Addendum to the running Interaction-Rules master list (§13C.5)

Add these from Part 13R:

> ⚡ **TWO GREEN-MANURE SYSTEMS:** *in-situ* (grown and buried in the same field — sunn hemp, dhaincha) versus *leaf manure* (trees grown on bunds, prunings carried into the field — Gliricidia, Sesbania sesban, karanj, subabul). **Leaf manure costs you NO cropping season** — the only system usable in an established perennial orchard.
> ⚡ **N contributed per acre:** Sesbania sesban **182 kg** ≫ cowpea **44** > sunn hemp **36.5** > dhaincha **31.5** > black gram/moth bean **29.75** > cluster bean **27** > berseem **17** > green gram **16**. Per tree: subabul **640 g**, Gliricidia **630 g**.
> ⚡ **Bury at FLOWERING, ~6–8 weeks after sowing.** Later burial raises the C:N ratio, increases fibre, and **slows decomposition**.
> ⚡ **Green manure draws nutrients from LOWER soil layers and releases them at the surface after burial** — a subsoil nutrient pump.
> ⚡ **Green manure increases availability of P, K, Ca, Mn and Fe; improves saline soils; and checks nematodes** — the three benefits that matter most on calcareous, salt-affected ground.
> ⚡ **BUT: continuous green manuring with the SAME species increases disease, pests and nematodes.** Rotate species. (Sunn hemp is genuinely nematode-suppressive; not all legumes are.)
> ⚡ **In low-rainfall areas green manure competes for the main crop's moisture AND may not decompose properly.** Sow with the monsoon; prefer intercropping over a dedicated season.
> ⚡ **Sunn hemp grows poorly in ACIDIC soils** (not a constraint on calcareous ground).

# PART 13 — Organic Carbon Management — PART S: OIL CAKES, MULCHING, COVER CROPS & MARKET PRODUCTS (Methods 7–10)

> Sources: 20 PPT slides (extracted from a Word document) + audio transcript (moderately corrupted; recovered from non-standard encoding). **This session completes the ten-method series.** Gore notes at the start that this is *"perhaps the last, or second-to-last webinar"* on organic carbon, after which the course moves to **soil testing → fertilizer types → correct usage → fertigation → foliar application**. ASR slips corrected inline: `पेंडी` = **oil cake**; `टरफलरहित/टरफलविरहित` = **de-hulled**; `कॉटन केक स्लरी` = **cotton cake slurry**; `आजादेरिटीन` = **azadirachtin**; `निमोलाइड्स` = **nimbolides/limonoids**; `सरकी` = **cottonseed**; `जवस` = **linseed**; `करडई` = **safflower**; `खोबरा` = **copra/coconut**; `मोहवा/महुआ` = **mahua**; `येरंड/एरंड` = **castor**; `आरक` = **extract**.

---

## 13S.1 METHOD 7 — VARIOUS OIL CAKES (विविध पेंडी)

### The definition and the two classes

> **तेलबियांमधून तेल काढल्यानंतर, उरलेला घन भाग पेंड म्हणून वाळवला जातो जो जनावरांना खाद्य तसेच खत म्हणूनही वापरता येतो.**
>
> *After oil is extracted from oilseeds, the remaining solid portion is dried as "cake," which can be used both as animal feed and as fertilizer.*

**पेंडी दोन प्रकारचे असतात — Oil cakes are of two types:**

| **Edible (पशूंना खाण्यायोग्य)** | **Non-edible (पशूंना खाण्यास योग्य नाही)** |
|---|---|
| Groundnut cake (शेंगदाणा पेंड), copra cake (खोबरा पेंड) etc. | Castor cake (एरंड पेंड), neem cake (निंबोळी पेंड), mahua cake (महुआ पेंड) etc. |

### The two key operational rules

> **खाण्यायोग्य आणि अखाद्य तेल पेंडी दोन्ही खत म्हणून वापरता येतात. तथापि, खाद्यतेल पेंडी गुरांना खायला दिले जातात आणि अखाद्य तेल पेंडी विशेषतः पिकांसाठी खत म्हणून वापरले जातात.**
>
> *Both edible and non-edible cakes can be used as fertilizer. However, edible cakes are generally fed to cattle, and **non-edible cakes are used specifically as crop fertilizer.***

> ⚡ **AVAILABILITY TIMING —** **तेलाच्या पेंडीमध्ये असलेले पोषक घटक जमिनीत टाकल्यानंतर 7 ते 10 दिवसांनी पिकांना उपलब्ध होतात.** — *the nutrients in oil cake become available to the crop **7 to 10 days after application to the soil.***

> ⚡ **PREPARATION RULE —** **पेंडीचे वितरण आणि जलद विघटन होण्यासाठी ते वापरण्यापूर्वी चांगले बारीक (पावडर) करणे आवश्यक असते.** — *for even distribution and rapid decomposition, **the cake must be well powdered before use.***

### The ten oil cakes shown

शेंगदाणा (groundnut) · जवस (linseed) · मोहरी (mustard) · तीळ (sesame) · एरंड (castor) · सरकी (cottonseed) · करंज (karanj) · महुवा (mahua) · करडई (safflower) · खोबरा (copra)

### THE COMPOSITION TABLE — nutrient content (%)

**Non-edible oil cakes (अखाद्य तेल-पेंडी)**

| Oil cake | N (%) | P (%) | K (%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **एरंड पेंड — Castor** | 4.3 | 1.8 | 1.3 |
| **सरकी पेंड (टरफलरहित) — Cottonseed (de-hulled)** | 3.9 | 1.8 | 1.6 |
| **करंज पेंड — Karanj** | 3.9 | 0.9 | 1.2 |
| **महुआ पेंड — Mahua** | 2.5 | 0.8 | 1.2 |
| **करडई पेंड (टरफलरहित) — Safflower (de-hulled)** | 4.9 | 1.4 | 1.2 |

**Edible oil cakes (खाद्यतेल-पेंडी)**

| Oil cake | N (%) | P (%) | K (%) |
|---|---|---|---|
| **खोबरा पेंड — Copra** | 3.0 | 1.9 | 1.8 |
| **सरकी पेंड (टरफलविरहित) — Cottonseed (de-hulled)** | 6.4 | 2.9 | 2.2 |
| **शेंगदाणा पेंड — Groundnut** | **7.3** | 1.5 | 1.3 |
| **जवस पेंड — Linseed** | 4.9 | 1.4 | 1.3 |
| **मोहरी पेंड — Mustard** | 5.2 | 1.8 | 1.2 |
| **करडई पेंड (टरफलविरहित) — Safflower (de-hulled)** | **7.9** | 2.2 | 1.9 |
| **तीळ पेंड — Sesame** | 6.2 | 2.0 | 1.2 |

> ⚡ **READING THE TABLE —** **edible cakes are markedly richer in nitrogen than non-edible cakes** (7.9%, 7.3%, 6.4% vs 4.3%, 3.9%). This is the economic tension: the high-nitrogen cakes are the ones worth feeding to cattle, which is why they cost more. The non-edible cakes (castor, neem, karanj, mahua) are cheaper as fertilizer precisely because nothing else wants them.

### Why oil MUST be removed — the mechanism

This is the technically important passage of the session:

> **Many people ask: why use only de-oiled cake? Why use the residue left after the oil is taken out?**
>
> ⚡ **THE REASON —** *"Whenever you put **any oil** into the soil, it can **lock up (साठवून ठेवू) some nutrients** in the soil, because **oil does not dissolve in water**, and it can carry some nutrients along with it. Will it hold them permanently? No. They will become available once the oil is fully decomposed in the soil. At that point its adverse effect stops."*

And the biological reason, which matters more:

> ⚡ **INTERACTION RULE —** *azadirachtin and the neem limonoids have **antifungal and antibacterial properties**. **If you apply neem cake WITH the oil still in it, the beneficial microorganisms in your soil will also develop in reduced numbers there.*** That is why, when Gore specifies neem cake in the **organic culture** recipe (Part 13K), he means **100% de-oiled** neem cake.

> ⚡ **HIS BLENDED-CAKE RECOMMENDATION —** *"When I explain the organic culture concept, I do not say take neem cake alone. **When taking neem cake it must be 100% oil-free, and a mixture of neem, karanj, castor and mahua should be used there**"* — a four-cake blend, available commercially.

### The neem chemistry — what remains after de-oiling

> **From neem oil we extract one major compound — of the various **nimbolides/limonoids** present in the neem seed (he cites having read of ~109 types) — one of which is **azadirachtin**, which gives insecticidal and some fungicidal properties. But **what remains behind stays in your cake, and that residue helps make soil fertile and supports the growth of living microbes.**

> **Note on neem's pest effect:** Gore says neem formulations do have real effects on soil beetles and their early-instar grubs (bitterness causes starvation; high azadirachtin concentration can kill young larvae), and some nematicidal effect — *"all these references exist."* **But once decomposition occurs in the soil, that insecticidal property is largely lost.** So neem cake's value in soil is as a **fertility and microbial input**, not primarily as a pesticide.

### The historical yield argument

> **"In Maharashtra, some farmers who still used oil cake were selling 100 tonnes of sugarcane. When chemicals started being used, that fell away. Which means the cakes were giving you production WHILE keeping the land fertile."**
>
> **The honest caveat:** *"But now the cost of cakes has risen so much, and getting that quantity is no longer possible — so we have to go towards chemicals."*

### The microbe-accelerator rule — genuinely useful

> ⚡ **SEQUENCING RULE —** *"If you want good results from any microorganism, **first put the oil cake there, and THEN use the microorganism**. You will get very good results from that microorganism. Your land will not fail to become fertile."*

This is the substrate-before-biology principle (Parts 13F, 13H) applied specifically: **oil cake is the ideal pre-charge for a microbial application** because it is protein-rich and decomposes fast.

> **His evidence:** *"Bring groundnut cake, and if the weather is rainy — just the atmospheric humidity absorbed by that cake, a little moisture — and within three or four days you will see fungus growing on it. Why? Because the **protein material** in them is their food, and they arrive there quickly."*

### Cotton cake slurry — the pomegranate colour formula

> ⚡ **HIS SPECIFIC RECOMMENDATION FOR POMEGRANATE —** **"Cotton Cake Slurry"** *(video on the BT Gore YouTube channel)*:
>
> - **Cottonseed cake (सरकी पेंड) + potash-mobilizing bacteria (KMB)**, fermented in a tank for **3 days**
> - Delivers *"size, shine, `[bloom]`, and disease resistance"*
> - **He calls it "an outstanding formula for colour development in pomegranate"** — the best results and the lowest cost of all the cakes he tested
> - **Re-extraction:** the same material can be soaked and re-fermented — *"take the extract, add more water and ferment again, extract again — do this about **four times** with the same material"* — and results remain very good

### The efficiency-booster rule

> ⚡ **RULE —** *"To increase the efficiency of **any chemical fertilizer** — granular fertilizer, single super phosphate, or water-soluble fertilizer — **you can use any type of oil cake.**"*

And his position against cake-snobbery:

> **"Many people say this type of cake should not be used, that cake doesn't work. I say: organic substances are given by nature itself. Use any type of cake for any crop."**

---

## 13S.2 METHOD 8 — ORGANIC MULCHING (सेंद्रिय मल्चिंग)

### The mulch materials shown

| Marathi | English |
|---|---|
| वूड चिप आच्छादन | **Wood chips** |
| शेंगदाण्याची टरफले | **Groundnut shells/husk** |
| झाडाची सालीचे आच्छादन | **Tree bark** |
| कापलेले गवत आच्छादन | **Cut grass** |
| वाळलेली पाने | **Dried leaves** |
| कोको बीन हुल्स | **Cocoa bean hulls** |
| लुसर्न आच्छादन | **Lucerne (alfalfa)** |
| पेंढा (Straw) | **Straw** |
| लाकडाचा भुसा | **Sawdust** |

*(Photographs also show sugarcane trash and orchard-floor mulch in pomegranate and mango.)*

### THE FOUR MULCHING CAUTIONS — मल्चिंग करताना घ्यायची काळजी

This is the most operationally important slide in the mulching section, and three of the four rules are ones most farmers get wrong:

| # | Caution |
|---|---|
| 1 | **मल्चिंग ही हिवाळ्यात करावी** — **Do mulching in WINTER** |
| 2 | **मल्चिंगचा थर 2 इंचापेक्षा जास्त नसावा, जमिनीमध्ये हवा खेळती राहायला हवी** — **The mulch layer must not exceed 2 INCHES; air must keep circulating in the soil** |
| 3 | **ठिबक सिंचन पद्धती असल्यास ठिबकची नळी मल्चिंगच्या खालीच ठेवाव्यात** — **If you have drip irrigation, keep the drip line UNDERNEATH the mulch** |
| 4 | **काळ्या जमिनीमध्ये पावसाळ्यात मल्चिंग काढून टाकावी** — **In BLACK soils, REMOVE the mulch during the monsoon** |

> ⚡ **WHY THE 2-INCH LIMIT MATTERS —** thicker mulch blocks air exchange, and per Part 13G's own teaching, **anaerobic conditions favour the harmful organisms** and suppress the beneficial aerobes. Mulch is meant to moderate temperature and conserve moisture, not to seal the soil.

> ⚡ **WHY DRIP GOES UNDER THE MULCH —** water must reach the soil, not evaporate off the mulch surface. A drip line laid *on top* of mulch wets the mulch and loses much of it to evaporation.

> ⚡ **WHY REMOVE MULCH FROM BLACK SOIL IN THE MONSOON —** black cotton soil already has poor drainage and high water-holding. Mulch on top during heavy rain keeps it waterlogged, which drives the root-rot and anaerobic problems of Chapters 6 and 12. *(Note: this is a black-soil rule. On light or calcareous soil the calculus differs.)*

---

## 13S.3 METHOD 9 — COVER CROPS (जमिनीला आच्छादित करणारी पिके)

### Definition and placement

> **मातीचे संरक्षण आणि समृद्ध करण्यासाठी जमिनीला आच्छादित करणारी पिकाची लागवड केली जाते.**
>
> *Cover crops are grown to protect and enrich the soil.*

> **जमिनीची धूप कमी करणे, तण नियंत्रित करणे, सेंद्रिय पदार्थ वाढवणे, नायट्रोजन स्थिर करणे आणि जमिनीत पाणी झिरपण्याचा दर सुधारून मातीचे आरोग्य आणि सुपीकता सुधारण्यासाठी सामान्यतः नियमित पिकांच्या दरम्यान किंवा पडीकच्या काळात ही पिके घेतली जातात.**
>
> *To reduce soil erosion, control weeds, increase organic matter, fix nitrogen, and improve the water-infiltration rate — thereby improving soil health and fertility — these crops are generally taken **between regular crops or during the fallow period.***

> **कव्हर क्रॉप हे एकात्मिक तण व्यवस्थापन, पिकांची फेरपालट, नांगरट न करता होणारी शेती, संवर्धन शेती, सेंद्रिय शेती, शाश्वत शेती पद्धती आणि मृदा आणि जलसंधारण पद्धतींमध्ये समाविष्ट असतात.**
>
> *Cover crops are integral to integrated weed management, crop rotation, **no-till farming**, conservation agriculture, organic farming, sustainable farming systems, and soil-and-water conservation practices.*

### The cover-crop species list

राई (rye) · हिवाळी गहू (winter wheat) · ज्वारी (sorghum) · बाजरी (pearl millet) · मका (maize) · वाटाणा (pea) · केसाळ वेच (hairy vetch) · बरसीम (berseem) · चवळी (cowpea) · मोहरी (mustard) · मुळा (radish) · सूर्यफूल (sunflower) · **झेंडू (marigold)** · हरभरा (chickpea) · भुईमूग (groundnut) · सुदान गवत (Sudan grass)

> **Note the title slide uses a marigold field** — marigold is a well-known nematode-suppressive cover crop, which connects to the nematode benefit below.

### THE SEVEN BENEFITS OF COVER CROPS

Each was given its own slide:

| # | Benefit (Marathi) | English |
|---|---|---|
| 1 | **रासायनिक खतांचा खर्च कमी करतात** | **Reduce the cost of chemical fertilizers** |
| 2 | **मातीची धूप कमी करतात** | **Reduce soil erosion** |
| 3 | **जमिनीचे भौतिक गुणधर्म जसे की पोत आणि पाणी धरून ठेवण्याची क्षमता सुधारणे** | **Improve the soil's physical properties — texture and water-holding capacity** |
| 4 | **तणनाशकांच्या खर्चात कपात करतात** | **Cut herbicide costs** (via the weed-suppression mechanisms: canopy shading, altered microclimate, allelochemicals, and residue as a physical barrier to weed-seedling emergence) |
| 5 | **कीटकांच्या समस्या कमी करतात** | **Reduce insect problems** (stronger predator recruitment; cover crops give predators habitat, and pest herbivory on the cash crop weakens) |
| 6 | **बुरशीनाशकांसारखे संयुगे जमिनीत सोडून मातीजन्य रोग कमी करतात** | **Release fungicide-like compounds into the soil, reducing soil-borne diseases** |
| 7 | **सूत्रकृमींना अटकाव** | **Check nematodes** — *"जर कव्हर क्रॉप सूत्रकृमीसाठी पर्यायी पीक नसेल तर सूत्रकृमी दुसऱ्या शेतात स्थलांतर करू शकत नाहीत"* — **if the cover crop is not an alternative host for the nematode, the nematodes cannot migrate to another field** |
| 8 | **जमिनीतील पाण्याचे प्रदूषण कमी करते** | **Reduce groundwater pollution** — *"जमिनीतील खते विशेषतः स्फुरदला खाली वाहून (Leach Out) जाण्यास अटकाव करते"* — **prevents soil fertilizers, especially phosphorus, from leaching out** |
| 9 | **जमिनीत गाडल्यानंतर जमिनीचा सेंद्रिय कर्ब वाढवण्यास मदत, परिणामी जमिनीची सुपीकता वाढते** | **After burial, helps increase soil organic carbon, and consequently soil fertility** |

> ⚡ **BENEFIT 7 — THE NEMATODE MECHANISM IS SUBTLE AND IMPORTANT.** A cover crop that is a **non-host** for the nematode acts as a biological dead-end: the nematodes cannot feed, cannot reproduce, and **cannot migrate onward.** This is why species selection matters — a legume cover crop that happens to be a good nematode host will make the problem *worse*, while marigold or a non-host grass breaks the cycle. *(This resolves the same tension flagged in Part 13R: green manure both "checks" and "increases" nematodes depending on species.)*

> ⚡ **BENEFIT 8 — THE PHOSPHORUS RETENTION POINT** is directly relevant on any soil where phosphorus is scarce or locked. Cover crops take up mobile nutrients that would otherwise leach below the root zone and hold them in living biomass until burial releases them.

---

## 13S.4 METHOD 10 — MARKET-AVAILABLE PRODUCTS (बाजारात उपलब्ध असलेले उत्पादने)

The final method's title slide shows what appears to be **biochar / charcoal-based material.** The session's audio does not detail this category before the transcript ends — Gore had indicated one further webinar would follow.

> **What this category means in context:** commercially available organic-carbon products — humic and fulvic acid preparations, biochar, seaweed extracts, and the "organic carbon" powders and granules he critiqued in Part 13C (*"companies only process it; organic carbon comes only from nature"*).
>
> **His standing test from Part 13C applies:** ask any such product's seller **"how many days does it work?"** — apply it, then measure organic carbon at 2, 10 and 15 days. If it rose and fell, that was dissolved organic matter giving a momentary lift, not humus.

> **"When I speak on chemicals, I will start that subject from SOIL TESTING. Then I will tell you the TYPES OF FERTILIZERS, their CORRECT USAGE — which fertilizer works with which, which does not; which fertilizer, when in excess, makes which other nutrient unavailable; what is needed to make which one available; how our FERTIGATION method should be; and how to use fertilizers through the leaves (FOLIAR APPLICATION). All these subjects we will look at from here on."**

# Analysis — Part 13S

## The oil-cake section is the strongest content here

**The de-oiling explanation is the technically important part, and it is correct.** Oil in soil genuinely does interfere — it is hydrophobic, coats particles, and slows both water movement and microbial access. And the specific point about **neem oil's azadirachtin suppressing beneficial microbes** is well-founded: azadirachtin and related limonoids have documented antifungal and antibacterial activity, so applying full-oil neem cake works against the very biology you are trying to build. **This retroactively clarifies the Part 13K organic-culture recipe — the 150 kg of neem cake must be de-oiled.**

**The composition table is genuinely useful and shows a clear pattern:** edible cakes (safflower 7.9% N, groundnut 7.3%, cottonseed 6.4%, sesame 6.2%) are far richer in nitrogen than non-edible ones (castor 4.3%, karanj 3.9%, mahua 2.5%). The economics follow directly — the rich ones have a feed market and cost more.

**The "oil cake first, then microbes" sequencing rule is practically valuable** and consistent with everything else in the course. Protein-rich cake is fast-decomposing microbial food; pre-charging with it before a biological application gives the inoculum something to eat immediately. His groundnut-cake-moulds-in-3-days observation is exactly the right evidence for it.

**The cotton cake slurry is his most specific pomegranate recommendation in the entire course** — cottonseed cake plus potash-mobilizing bacteria, 3-day ferment, for colour and size. Worth noting: it is also consistent with the Chapter 11 teaching that potassium drives fruit colour and size, and with the Part 13O warning that colour is partly climatic. **Treat it as a genuine candidate to trial, not a guaranteed fix.**

## The mulching cautions are the most actionable thing in the session

All four are correct and three are commonly violated:

- **2-inch maximum** — thicker mulch goes anaerobic, which contradicts the whole aerobic-biology programme
- **Drip line under the mulch** — otherwise you irrigate the mulch, not the soil
- **Remove from black soil in monsoon** — prevents waterlogging on soil that already drains poorly
- **Mulch in winter** — the seasonal timing

## Cautions and corrections

| Item | Assessment |
|---|---|
| **"Do mulching in winter"** | Correct for Maharashtra's purpose (moderating winter soil temperature and conserving moisture into summer). **But on a hot semi-arid site the strongest argument for mulch is summer heat and evaporation control** (Parts 13E, 13G). Do not read this as "remove mulch in summer" — that would be exactly wrong for Pulivendula. |
| **"Remove mulch in monsoon"** | Stated for **black soils** specifically. On calcareous, lighter, better-draining soil the waterlogging risk is lower and the rule may not apply. Judge by your own drainage. |
| **Sawdust listed as a mulch** | Consistent as *surface* mulch, but recall Part 13G's strong warning: **never incorporate sawdust into soil** — highest C:N ratio of all, causes severe nitrogen immobilisation. On the surface it is acceptable; tilled in it is not. |
| **"Use any type of cake for any crop"** | Reasonable as a rebuttal to cake-snobbery, but note **castor and mahua cakes are toxic to livestock** (ricin in castor; saponins in mahua) — safe as fertilizer, dangerous if animals access the store. |
| **The 100-tonne sugarcane claim** | Anecdotal and uncontrolled; also self-undercut by his own admission that cake quantities are no longer economically available. |
| **Cotton cake slurry "4 times re-extraction"** | Plausible but unverified; each extraction will yield progressively less. |
| **Method 10 not covered** | The market-products category was only introduced. Apply his own Part 13C turnover-time test to anything sold in this category. |

## For Pulivendula

1. **Use de-oiled cake, and prefer the four-cake blend.** For your organic-culture batches (Part 13K) and general soil application, insist on **100% de-oiled** material, and use the **neem + karanj + castor + mahua** blend Gore recommends rather than neem alone. The non-edible cakes are also the cheaper option, since they have no feed market competing for them.

2. **Sequence cake before biology.** Apply oil cake first, then your microbial input (IEC slurry, EM, Waste Decomposer) 7–10 days later. The cake gives the inoculum immediate food, and the nutrients become plant-available on roughly the same timescale.

3. **Trial the cotton cake slurry at your first bahar.** Cottonseed cake + KMB, 3-day ferment. It targets colour and size — the two traits that determine your price — and it is cheap. But hold the Part 13O caveat in mind: **anthocyanin colour is substantially climatic**, so do not expect it to overcome an extreme-heat ripening window.

4. **Mulching rules, adapted to your site.** Keep to the **2-inch maximum** (air matters more than bulk) and put the **drip line under the mulch**. But invert his seasonal advice: on a 45 °C semi-arid block, **mulch is most valuable through the hot months** — for evaporation control and the capillary salt-rise suppression from Part 13G. The "remove in monsoon" rule applies to black cotton soil; on your lighter calcareous ground, judge by whether water is actually standing.

5. **Cover crops are your inter-row system — choose non-host species deliberately.** Benefit 7 is the one to design around: a **non-host** cover crop starves nematodes and blocks their migration, while a host species multiplies them. **Marigold** (on the title slide) is the classic nematode-suppressive choice and would be a strong inter-row option for a Bhagwa block where root-knot is a named risk. Sorghum/Sudan grass is another.

6. **Benefit 8 matters on your soil specifically** — cover crops prevent phosphorus leaching. On calcareous ground where phosphorus is already locked and scarce, retaining what you have in living biomass and releasing it on burial is worth more than on a fertile soil.

7. **Never till sawdust in.** Surface mulch only, if at all.

---

## Addendum to the running Interaction-Rules master list (§13C.5)

Add these from Part 13S:

> ⚡ **Oil-cake nutrients become plant-available 7–10 days after soil application.** Powder the cake before use for even distribution and fast decomposition.
> ⚡ **Oil in soil locks up nutrients** (hydrophobic, doesn't dissolve) — use **de-oiled cake only**. And **azadirachtin in full-oil neem cake suppresses beneficial soil microbes**, defeating the purpose.
> ⚡ **Edible cakes are far richer in N** (safflower 7.9%, groundnut 7.3%, cottonseed 6.4%) **than non-edible** (castor 4.3%, karanj 3.9%, mahua 2.5%) — which is why the non-edible ones are cheaper as fertilizer.
> ⚡ **SEQUENCING: apply oil cake FIRST, then the microbial inoculum.** Protein-rich cake is immediate microbial food and multiplies inoculum results.
> ⚡ **Oil cake increases the efficiency of ANY chemical fertilizer** — granular, SSP or water-soluble.
> ⚡ **MULCH ≤ 2 INCHES** — thicker blocks air and turns the soil anaerobic, favouring harmful organisms.
> ⚡ **Drip line goes UNDER the mulch**, never on top.
> ⚡ **In BLACK soils, remove mulch during the monsoon** to prevent waterlogging.
> ⚡ **Cover crops check nematodes ONLY if they are a NON-HOST** — a host species multiplies them instead. Non-host cover crops also block nematode migration to adjacent fields.
> ⚡ **Cover crops prevent phosphorus from leaching below the root zone** — holding it in biomass until burial.
> ⚡ **Cover crops release fungicide-like compounds (allelochemicals)** that reduce soil-borne disease, and suppress weeds by shading, microclimate change and physical residue barrier.
